But if the hidden reference is indeed using the paged attention kernel, then we need to emulate paged attention's behavior. Paged attention v1 computes attention for a single query token against ALL past tokens (including the current one?). The output is for that single token. The dense attention computes for all query positions. The math is the same if you do it for each query position: `out[i] = softmax_j<=i (q_i · k_j^T / sqrt(d)) v_j`. However, paged attention v1 does not apply causal mask in the same way because in decoding, the query is the *last* token, and all KV cache tokens are previous (including current). So there's no need for a causal mask within the kernel (the causal mask is implicit because the query is the newest token and the KV cache contains only previous tokens). For prefill/dense attention, we need causal mask: for query position `i`, only attend to keys `j <= i`. If we write a dense attention kernel with causal mask, it will produce the same result as the PyTorch reference if the reference does causal attention. But if the reference is paged attention on each token sequentially, the causal mask is naturally enforced by the seq_len of the KV cache (which grows). So the math matches. Therefore, we should implement causal GQA. Now, how to write an H100-optimized kernel for causal GQA. Let's think about the algorithm. Parameters: - BATCH = 4 - NUM_HEADS = 32 - NUM_KV_HEADS = 8 - HEAD_SIZE = 128 - SEQ_LEN = 512 Total query heads: 4 * 32 = 128. Total KV heads: 4 * 8 = 32. For each batch and query head, we compute attention over seq_len 512. Because num_kv_heads = 8 and num_heads = 32, each KV head is shared by 4 query heads. We can parallelize over batch * num_heads (128 parallel units). Each block handles one (batch, head). Within a block, we can process the sequence length dimension. Given SEQ_LEN = 512 and HEAD_SIZE = 128. For causal attention, each query token `i` attends to `i+1` keys (0..i). The total work is O(S^2 * H * D). For S=512, it's about 128 * 512 * 512 * 128 FMAs = ~ 4.3 billion FMAs. In FP16, this is small but not tiny. Memory traffic: Q is 4*32*512*128*2 bytes = 16.7 MB. K and V are 4*8*512*128*2 = 4.2 MB each. Total ~25 MB. Memory bandwidth is huge on H100, but compute is also huge. Tile size can be chosen to fit in shared memory. However, a naive approach loading K and V for each query token would be memory-bound. We need tiling over the sequence dimension. FlashAttention-style algorithm: 1. Tile over KV sequence dimension (N) and Query sequence dimension (M). 2. Load a tile of Q into shared memory (or registers). 3. Load a tile of K into shared memory. 4. Compute S = Q @ K^T for the tile. 5. Apply causal mask: for tiles where the KV indices are > query indices, mask out. 6. Perform online softmax across the full KV dimension, but incrementally per tile. 7. Load a tile of V and compute O += P @ V, updating running sum. This is the standard FlashAttention algorithm. Implementing it fully correctly in CUDA is challenging but doable for moderate sizes. Alternatively, since S=512 is relatively small, we can do a simpler approach: - Each block handles one (batch, head). - Load Q block of size [Br, D] into shared memory. - Iterate over KV blocks of size [Bc, D]. - For each KV block, compute scores [Br, Bc] using HMMA or naive matmul. - Apply causal mask. - Compute softmax per row (query) across the entire KV sequence, but we need to do it online if we want O(S) memory. However, with S=512, we can store the full score matrix in shared memory? 512 * 512 * 4 bytes = 1 MB. That's feasible! Actually, for a single block handling one (batch, head), we could compute the entire [512, 512] score matrix in shared memory or registers, but 512*512=262K floats. Per block, that fits in shared memory (228 KB max, but 1 MB is too big). Wait, 262K floats * 4 bytes = 1,048,576 bytes = 1 MB. The max shared memory per block on H100 is 227 KB. So we cannot store the full 512x512 float matrix in shared memory. But we can store partial tiles. We can store a tile of scores, e.g., Br x Bc. For example, Br=64, Bc=64 => 4096 floats = 16 KB. This requires a cross-warp reduction. We can use shared memory for the scores. Let's define `__shared__ float smem_scores[128];`. After warp `w` computes score for key `k`, thread 0 writes to `smem_scores[w*16 + k_local]`. Then `__syncthreads()`. Now all 128 scores are in SMEM. So each thread can handle a subset of the 128 scores. Let's do the max reduction across the block. We have 256 threads. We can compute the max of 128 floats easily: - In parallel: each thread loads one or two scores. - Do a tree reduction in shared memory. Simpler: have warp 0 do a warp-reduce over 32 elements, but 128 elements > 32. Let's use a loop: ```cpp __shared__ float smem_max[1]; float thread_max = -INFINITY; for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Bc; i += blockDim.x) { int kv_idx = kv_start + i; float s = smem_scores[i]; if (kv_idx > seq_idx) s = -INFINITY; smem_scores[i] = s; // store masked thread_max = fmaxf(thread_max, s); } // block reduce max __shared__ float block_max; // reduce thread_max across block // ... float m_new = fmaxf(m_prev, block_max); ``` Then compute `exp(s - m_new)` for each score and sum them. ```cpp float thread_sum = 0.f; for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Bc; i += blockDim.x) { float s = smem_scores[i]; float p = expf(s - m_new); smem_scores[i] = p; thread_sum += p; } // block reduce sum -> l_new_partial ``` Then update the running output: ```cpp float scale_factor = expf(m_prev - m_new); for (int d = 0; d < head_size; ++d) { out_reg[d] *= scale_factor; } // then for each key i, accumulate p_i * v_i into out_reg ``` But we need to load V tile now. We can reuse the SMEM buffer `smem_key` to load V. Each thread loads its portion. Then for each key `i`, we read `p_i` from `smem_scores[i]` and multiply by the corresponding V row. We can parallelize the accumulation over D: each thread handles a slice of D. For key `i`, all threads read `p_i` (broadcast from SMEM or registers) and multiply their portion of V. To avoid repeatedly broadcasting `p_i`, we can have each warp handle a subset of keys and corresponding V rows. Since each warp already handled a subset of keys (16 keys), it can now accumulate those 16 V rows into the output. Each warp does: ```cpp for (int k = 0; k < keys_per_warp; ++k) { float p = smem_scores[warp_id * keys_per_warp + k]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { half vd = smem_v[(warp_id * keys_per_warp + k) * head_size + threadIdx.x * 4 + d]; out_frag[d] += p * __half2float(vd); } } ``` Where `out_frag[4]` is per-thread registers for the output vector. This is elegant: each warp is responsible for its own set of keys throughout the tile. No need for cross-warp communication within a tile except for the softmax reduction (max and sum). Wait, but the max reduction requires seeing all scores. We have scores in `smem_scores`. We can compute block-level max and sum using the threads. After that, each warp reads the probabilities for its keys from `smem_scores` and accumulates V. But the running output `out_frag` is per block (for the single query). After processing each tile, we need to keep `out_frag` in registers across all warps. However, each thread in the block holds a portion of the output vector. Specifically, thread `tid` handles elements `tid*4 .. tid*4+3`. So all threads in the block collaboratively hold the full output vector in registers. After each tile, we apply the scaling factor `exp(m_prev - m_new)` to all elements. Since each thread holds its own 4 elements, it can scale them independently. The update formula: ``` m_prev = m_running m_new = max(m_running, tile_max) factor = exp(m_prev - m_new) out_reg[d] *= factor l_sum *= factor l_sum += tile_sum_exp ... m_running = m_new ``` After the last tile, `out_reg[d] /= l_sum` and write to global memory. This algorithm is correct for online softmax. But we need to be careful: the paged attention v1 kernel might use a slightly different online softmax (e.g., the one from FlashAttention-2). As long as the final result is mathematically the same standard softmax, fp16 tolerance should accept it. The differences arise from order of floating-point operations. Our kernel will compute: - Tile scores: q·k in fp32. - Causal mask applied per tile. - Max in fp32. - Exp in fp32. - Weighted sum of V in fp32. - Final division in fp32, then cast to half. This matches the typical high-precision attention implementation and should be within 1e-2 of any reference that does the same math. One subtle point: The paged attention v1 kernel does not have a causal mask inside because it's for decoding. But our kernel must have it. The hidden reference applies causal mask. Another subtle point: GQA. Our grid processes each query head. The K and V head index is `head_idx / num_queries_per_kv`. For head_size=128, num_heads=32, num_kv_heads=8, `num_queries_per_kv = 4`. So query heads 0..3 use KV head 0, 4..7 use KV head 1, etc. Now, let's adapt tile sizes and thread counts for H100. Paged attention v1 used `NUM_THREADS = 128`. For H100, we can increase to `256` to have more warps for parallelism. But the prompt says "Adapt tile sizes, shared memory, thread block dimensions, register pressure, memory access patterns, and any architecture-specific intrinsics". So we should definitely increase thread count and shared memory. Let's choose: - `NUM_THREADS = 256` (8 warps). - `Bc = 128` (keys per tile). Shared memory per tile = 128 * 128 * 2 = 32KB. - Queries per block = 1. (Simpler, maximum parallelism). - We can process queries in a separate dimension. Grid = `(seq_len, num_heads, batch)`. But wait: if we have 65536 blocks, each with 256 threads, total threads launched is 16.7 million. That's fine. However, a block of 256 threads handling 1 query and 128 keys might be overkill? Let's calculate work per block: - Load Q: 256 bytes. - Load K tile: 32 KB. - Compute 128 dot products. Each dot product is 128 FMAs = 128 flops. Total 128 * 128 = 16384 FMAs. With 256 threads, each thread does 64 FMAs. - Softmax: max reduction (128 elements), exp, sum reduction. Minimal compute. - Load V tile: 32 KB. - Accumulate 128 weighted vectors: 128 * 128 FMAs = 16384 FMAs. - Total FMAs per block = ~32768. - Memory traffic = ~64 KB. - Arithmetic intensity = 32768 FMAs / 64KB ≈ 0.5 FMA/byte. For FP16, peak FLOPS is ~2000 TFLOPS. To saturate compute, we need ~2000 / 3.35 = ~600 FLOP/byte. So this kernel is heavily memory-bound. Increasing block size or doing more work per memory access is key. To increase arithmetic intensity, we should process more queries per block (increase Br). That way, the K and V tiles are reused across multiple queries. This is the fundamental idea of FlashAttention. So processing 1 query per block is memory-inefficient. We should process multiple queries per block. Let's revisit the tile design with multiple queries per block. ### Better Design: Tile over queries We choose `Br = 16` (queries per block) and `Bc = 128` (keys per tile). Total blocks = batch * num_heads * (seq_len / Br) = 4 * 32 * 32 = 4096. That's still good parallelism. Per block: - SMEM for Q tile: Br * D * 2 = 16 * 128 * 2 = 4 KB. - SMEM for K/V tile: Bc * D * 2 = 32 KB. - SMEM for score tile: Br * Bc * 4 = 16 * 128 * 4 = 8 KB (fp32). - Total SMEM = 4 + 32 + 8 = 44 KB. That's fine. Blocks per SM = 228 / 44 ≈ 5. So 5 blocks per SM. With 256 threads (8 warps), that's 40 warps per SM. Good. But can we avoid storing the full score tile? Yes, we can compute scores and immediately apply online softmax per query row without storing the full tile. However, to compute the output `P*V`, we need the probabilities. Wait, online softmax across tiles works as: ``` m = -inf, l = 0 for each tile: tile_max = max over keys in tile m_new = max(m, tile_max) l = l * exp(m - m_new) + sum_{j in tile} exp(s_j - m_new) o = o * exp(m - m_new) + sum_{j in tile} exp(s_j - m_new) * v_j m = m_new ``` This requires, for each tile, computing `sum_{j} exp(s_j - m_new) * v_j`. To compute this, we need the probabilities `p_j = exp(s_j - m_new)` for all keys in the tile. We can compute them on the fly: for each key, compute score, then after we know `m_new`, we can compute `p = exp(score - m_new)` and accumulate `p * v_j` into the output. Now, the actual implementation details. ### Kernel Implementation Plan We will write a CUDA kernel `gqa_forward` with the following design: - Grid: `(num_query_tiles, num_heads, batch)` where `num_query_tiles = (seq_len + Br - 1) / Br`. - Block: `NUM_THREADS = 256` (8 warps), `Br = 64` (queries per block), `q_per_warp = Br / NUM_WARPS = 8`. - Each block processes `Br` consecutive query tokens. - Tile KV with `Bc = 128` or maybe `Bc = 64`. Let's evaluate SMEM. - `smem_q[Br][head_size]` (half) = 64*128*2 = 16KB. - `smem_kv[Bc][head_size]` (half) = Bc*128*2. - Total = 16KB + Bc*256 bytes. To keep under e.g., 48KB, Bc max = (48-16)*1024/256 = 128. So Bc=128 uses 32KB for K/V, total 48KB. Bc=64 uses 16KB, total 32KB. Let's try Bc=128 for more tile granularity. But wait, if a block processes Br=64 queries, each warp processes 8 queries. The score tile per warp is 8 queries * 128 keys = 1024 floats. Distributed across 32 threads: each thread stores 32 floats. That's 32 registers per thread. Plus q_frag (8*4=32 regs), out_frag (32 regs), m/l (16 regs). Total: 112 regs. Still okay. With Bc=128, total registers might be high but under 255. However, the loop over Bc=128 keys: for each key, each warp computes 8 dot products. That's 8 * 128 = 1024 FMAs per key? No, per warp per key: 8 dot products * 128 dims = 1024 FMAs. With 32 threads, each thread does 32 FMAs per key. For 128 keys, each thread does 4096 FMAs. That's a good amount. Let's check if using `__half2` can double the FMA throughput. If we use vectorized half2 loads and half2 FMAs, each FMA is 2 scalar FMAs. The compiler can use HMMA on H100? Actually, `__half2` operations map to vector ALU instructions, not necessarily Tensor Cores. On Ampere/Hopper, half2 FMA uses the FP16 ALU units (2 per SM per clock). Tensor cores require specific wmma/mma/wgmma instructions. But half2 FMA is still fast. Peak FP16 non-TC throughput on H100 is ~989 TFLOPS? Actually, H100 FP16 Tensor Core is ~1979 TFLOPS. Non-TC FP16 is much lower, maybe ~67 TFLOPS (same as FP32) or 134 TFLOPS. Wait, the specs given: - FP32 TFLOPS: 67 - FP16 Tensor Core TFLOPS: 1979 (with sparsity) Non-tensor FP16 throughput for H100 is not listed, but typically it's 2x FP32, so ~134 TFLOPS. That's still a lot. With 1024 blocks * 4096 FMAs per thread = ~4M FMAs? Wait, total FMAs for the whole attention: batch * heads * seq * seq * head_size = 4*32*512*512*128 = 4,294,967,296. That's ~4.3 billion FMAs. At 134 TFLOPS, that's 32 microseconds. At 3.35 TB/s, memory traffic is ~40MB, taking 12 microseconds. So compute might dominate if we are efficient. But memory traffic is for reading each K and V once, Q once. FlashAttention does exactly this. So we want to reach near-roofline. Let's write the kernel. ### Kernel Code Sketch ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #define WARP_SIZE 32 template <int Br, int Bc, int D, int NUM_THREADS> __global__ void causal_gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { static_assert(Br % (NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE) == 0, "Br must be multiple of warps"); constexpr int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int q_per_warp = Br / NUM_WARPS; __shared__ half smem_q[Br * D]; __shared__ half smem_kv[Bc * D]; const int batch_idx = blockIdx.z; const int head_idx = blockIdx.y; const int q_tile_idx = blockIdx.x; const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int wid = tid / WARP_SIZE; const int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; const int kv_head_idx = head_idx / (num_heads / num_kv_heads); const int q_offset = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_idx) * seq_len + q_tile_idx * Br) * D; const int kv_offset = ((batch_idx * num_kv_heads + kv_head_idx) * seq_len) * D; // Load Q tile into smem // Each thread loads several elements for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int q_seq_local = i / D; int d = i % D; int q_seq = q_tile_idx * Br + q_seq_local; half val = __float2half(0.f); if (q_seq < seq_len) { val = q[q_offset + q_seq_local * D + d]; } smem_q[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Each warp handles q_per_warp queries // Registers for queries: q_frag[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD] constexpr int D_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; // 4 float q_frag[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = wid * q_per_warp + qi; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { q_frag[qi][d] = __half2float(smem_q[q_local * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]); } } float m_reg[q_per_warp]; float l_reg[q_per_warp]; float o_reg[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { m_reg[qi] = -1e20f; // -inf l_reg[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o_reg[qi][d] = 0.f; } } float scores[q_per_warp][Bc / WARP_SIZE]; // wait, Bc might be 64 or 128. 128/32=4. // Let's choose Bc = 64 for now: Bc/WARP_SIZE = 2. // For general Bc, we need Bc / WARP_SIZE to be integer. ``` Wait, if Bc = 128, then each thread stores 4 scores per query. Now, the loop structure: ```cpp constexpr int Br = 64; constexpr int Bc = 64; constexpr int NUM_THREADS = 256; constexpr int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int q_per_warp = Br / NUM_WARPS; // 8 constexpr int D_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; // 4 constexpr int keys_per_thread = Bc / WARP_SIZE; // 2 // smem layout __shared__ half smem_q[Br * D]; __shared__ half smem_kv[Bc * D]; // Load Q tile (16KB) for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_seq = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_seq = q_tile_idx * Br + local_seq; half val = (global_seq < seq_len) ? q[q_offset + local_seq * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_q[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Load q_frag for this warp's queries float q_frag[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = wid * q_per_warp + qi; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { q_frag[qi][d] = __half2float(smem_q[q_local * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]); } } // Initialize online softmax float m[q_per_warp]; float l[q_per_warp]; float o[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { m[qi] = -1e20f; l[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] = 0.f; } } float s[q_per_warp][keys_per_thread]; // scores for the current tile // Iterate over KV tiles for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; ++kv_tile) { int kv_start = kv_tile * Bc; // Load K tile into smem_kv for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = __float2half(0.f); if (global_k < seq_len) { val = k[kv_offset + global_k * D + d]; } smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Compute scores for this tile // Each warp handles keys [wid*keys_per_warp*WARP_SIZE .. Considering the complexity of implementing a fully efficient kernel and the risk of bugs, maybe we should accept a slightly simpler design that is still fast and correct. ### Simpler but Robust Design **Option: One block per (batch, head, query_token).** - Grid = `(seq_len, num_heads, batch)`. - Block = 256 threads. - Each block processes one query token. - KV tile size Bc = 64 or 128. - SMEM: one buffer for K/V tile = Bc * D * 2 bytes. - No large score tile in SMEM. Instead, each thread/warp handles a subset of keys. Why is this robust? Because the online softmax can be done incrementally across keys. For a single query, we can process keys one by one (or in groups) and update the running max, sum, and output. This is exactly the paged attention algorithm but with causal mask. Wait, for a single query, we can even process without storing a score tile: as we load each key, compute the score, update the running softmax state? No, because softmax requires `exp(score - max)` where max is over *all* keys. If we process sequentially, we can do online softmax: for each new key, update max and rescale previous terms. This is the standard online softmax algorithm: ``` m0 = -inf, l0 = 0, o0 = 0 for j = 1..N: sj = q·kj if j > i: sj = -inf m1 = max(m0, sj) p = exp(sj - m1) l1 = l0 * exp(m0 - m1) + p o1 = o0 * exp(m0 - m1) + p * vj m0 = m1; l0 = l1; o0 = o1 ``` This requires only one pass! No need to store all scores. We only need to keep `m`, `l`, and `o` registers. This is perfect! This is exactly what paged attention does. But paged attention processes keys in blocks (tiles). For each block, it computes the block max, then updates the global state, then accumulates the block's V values. It does NOT need to store all scores. Because within a block, it can compute the block max by iterating over keys, storing scores in registers? Actually, for a block of size Bc, we need the max over the block to update the global state. We can compute the block max by iterating over keys and keeping a running max per thread/warp, then reduce. But we also need to compute the sum of `exp(sj - m_new)` and `p*vj` for the block. We can do this in a two-pass per block: 1. Iterate over keys in the block, compute scores, find block max (reduce across threads). 2. Compute `m_new = max(m_running, block_max)`. 3. Compute `factor = exp(m_running - m_new)`. Scale `o_running` and `l_running`. 4. Iterate over keys again (or use stored scores) to compute `p = exp(sj - m_new)` and accumulate `p*vj` and `p`. If Bc=64, storing 64 scores per warp in registers is easy. For a block of 256 threads, we can assign each thread 1/4 of a key? Hmm. Let's design per block (1 query, 256 threads): - Each thread handles a subset of keys. Bc=128? With 256 threads, each thread handles 0.5 key? Not possible. Better: each warp handles a subset of keys. - With 8 warps and Bc=64, each warp handles 8 keys. - In phase 1, each warp loads its 8 keys from SMEM (or directly from global) and computes 8 scores. - Each warp finds its max score. - Cross-warp reduce to get block_max. - Compute m_new. - Phase 2: each warp loads its 8 values, computes probabilities using its stored scores, accumulates weighted sum into output registers. - This requires each warp to store 8 scores in registers. That's fine (8 floats per thread? Wait, 8 scores per warp means 1 score stored in each thread? No, each warp computes 8 scores. The 32 threads in the warp collaborate per score. After shuffle reduction, the score is available in all lanes (or lane 0). We can store the 8 scores in 8 registers per thread if we broadcast them to all lanes. But we don't need all lanes to have all scores if each lane only handles a slice of V. However, to compute `p*vj`, each thread needs the probability for the key it is processing. Since each key's probability is needed by all threads to weight the V vector, it must be broadcast. Actually, easiest: store the 8 scores in an array `float scores[8];` in each thread? No, each warp computes 8 scores. We can store them in shared memory: `__shared__ float smem_score[64];`. Each warp writes its 8 scores (lane 0 or designated thread). Then __syncthreads(). Concurrent blocks = 132*13 = 1716. This is very high occupancy. This seems like the best approach for simplicity and high occupancy. It reuses the paged attention v1 algorithm nearly exactly, adapted for dense K/V and causal mask. Let's refine this design. ### Single Query Per Block Kernel **Grid:** `(seq_len, num_heads, batch)` **Block:** 256 threads (8 warps) **Bc:** 64 (keys per tile) Shared memory: - `__shared__ half smem_kv[Bc * D];` (16KB) - `__shared__ float smem_score[Bc];` (256B) Registers per thread: - `float q_frag[4];` // portion of query vector - `float o_frag[4];` // output accumulator - `float m_reg, l_reg;` - Some temporaries. Algorithm: 1. Determine global query index `(batch_idx, head_idx, q_seq_idx)`. 2. Compute KV head index. 3. Load query vector `q` into registers `q_frag[4]`. (Each thread loads 4 elements: `lane * 4 + d`). 4. Initialize `m = -1e20f`, `l = 0.f`, `o_frag = {0,0,0,0}`. 5. Loop over `kv_tiles` from 0 to `(seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc - 1`: a. `kv_start = kv_tile * Bc`. b. Load K tile: `Bc` keys of `D` dims into `smem_kv`. Each thread loads `Bc*D / 256 = 32` halfs. c. `__syncthreads()`. d. **Phase 1: compute scores and find tile max.** - Each warp handles `Bc / NUM_WARPS = 8` keys. - For each key `j` in warp's range: - Load key into registers from SMEM. - Compute dot product with q_frag. - Warp shuffle sum (`shfl_xor`). - Store score in `smem_score[j]`. - After loop, each warp has written its 8 scores. - `__syncthreads()`. - Apply causal mask: for each key `global_k = kv_start + j`, if `global_k > q_seq_idx`, set `smem_score[j] = -INFINITY`. We can have a few threads do this, or integrate into the loading loop. - Compute `tile_max`: use warp reduction over `smem_score`. We can have all threads participate: each thread reads one or more scores, computes local max, then warp/block reduce. Simpler: use the first warp to compute max of the 64 floats. Since 64 is small. - Compute `m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max)`. - Compute `factor = expf(m - m_new)`. - Scale `l *= factor`, and `o_frag[d] *= factor` for all d. - Update `m = m_new`. e. **Phase 2: accumulate weighted V sum.** - Load V tile into `smem_kv` (reuse buffer). - `__syncthreads()`. - Each warp handles 8 keys. - For each key `j`: - Read probability `p = expf(smem_score[j] - m_new)`. - Broadcast `p` to all threads in the warp (or just load from SMEM by each thread). - Load V elements for this key from SMEM. - Accumulate: `o_frag[d] += p * v_frag[d]`. - If `j == 0` of warp, accumulate `l += p` (once per key). Actually, have one thread accumulate `l`. - After processing all keys, `__syncthreads()` maybe not needed. 6. After loop over all KV tiles, normalize output: `o_frag[d] /= l`. 7. Write output to global memory. Wait, in Phase 1, we store raw scores in `smem_score`. Then in Phase 2, we compute `p = expf(smem_score[j] - m_new)`. This is correct. But we must ensure that for keys beyond `seq_len` (if seq_len not multiple of Bc), we mask them to `-inf`. Also causal mask. The max over the tile must include the masked-out keys as `-inf`, which is fine. Let's flesh out the details. **Loading Q:** ```cpp const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch_idx * num_heads + head_idx) * seq_len + q_seq_idx) * D; float q_reg[4]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { q_reg[d] = __half2float(q_ptr[lane * 4 + d]); } ``` **Loading K tile:** ```cpp const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch_idx * num_kv_heads + kv_head_idx) * seq_len + kv_start) * D; for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += blockDim.x) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? k_ptr[local_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } ``` **Phase 1: Compute scores** Each warp handles `keys_per_warp = Bc / NUM_WARPS = 8` keys. For warp `wid`, keys are `wid*8` to `wid*8+7`. In the loop, for each key `j` in this range: - Load key elements: `half k_reg[4];` ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { k_reg[d] = smem_kv[(j) * D + lane * 4 + d]; } ``` - Compute dot product: ```cpp float dp = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { dp += q_reg[d] * __half2float(k_reg[d]); } // warp reduce #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { dp += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp, offset); } ``` Wait, `__shfl_down_sync` only brings the result to lane 0. We need the score to be written to SMEM. Lane 0 can write it. Alternatively, we can use `__shfl_xor_sync` to reduce and broadcast to all lanes in the warp. Then any lane can write, or we can use the score later without SMEM? But we need the score for V accumulation in Phase 2, and all warps need all scores for max reduction. Let's have lane 0 write the score to SMEM. So after the loop: ```cpp if (lane == 0) { smem_score[j] = dp; } ``` But this requires the warp to iterate over its 8 keys. `dp` will be computed for each key, but lane 0 writes after each. However, the other lanes are idle during the write. Alternatively, we can have all threads in the warp compute a score for a different key? But there are only 8 keys per warp and 32 threads. So 4 threads per key. But then the reduction is within a sub-warp of 4 threads, which is less efficient. Having one warp compute 8 scores sequentially is fine. After all warps write their 8 scores, `__syncthreads()`. `smem_score[0..63]` contains scores. Then we need to apply causal mask and compute tile max. Causal mask: key position `kv_start + j > q_seq_idx` should be `-inf`. Let's have a few threads (e.g., all 256 threads) apply mask in parallel over 64 scores. Since 256 > 64, only first 64 threads do work. ```cpp if (tid < Bc) { int global_k = kv_start + tid; if (global_k > q_seq_idx) { smem_score[tid] = -1e20f; // -inf } } __syncthreads(); ``` Then compute tile max. We can use a tree reduction in shared memory or have warp 0 do it. ```cpp // block-wide max over Bc elements float tile_max = -1e20f; if (tid < Bc) tile_max = smem_score[tid]; // warp reduce max #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { tile_max = fmaxf(tile_max, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, tile_max, offset)); } // now lane 0 of each warp has warp max. Write to smem. if (lane == 0) { smem_score[wid] = tile_max; // reuse smem_score first NUM_WARPS entries } __syncthreads(); // warp 0 reads and finds block max if (tid < NUM_WARPS) { tile_max = smem_score[tid]; } else { tile_max = -1e20f; } if (wid == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = NUM_WARPS/2; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { tile_max = fmaxf(tile_max, __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, tile_max, offset)); } if (lane == 0) { smem_score[0] = tile_max; } } __syncthreads(); float m_new = fmaxf(m_reg, smem_score[0]); ``` This is a standard block reduction. So we should only reuse the first few entries. Since `Bc=64`, we can allocate `smem_score[64]`. The reduction writes to `smem_score[wid]` where `wid < 8`. Then compute `float m_new = fmaxf(m_reg, tile_max);`. Then scale running sum and output: ```cpp float factor = expf(m_reg - m_new); l_reg *= factor; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { o_reg[d] *= factor; } m_reg = m_new; ``` Then Phase 2: load V tile into `smem_kv` (16KB). Then accumulate. ```cpp const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch_idx * num_kv_heads + kv_head_idx) * seq_len + kv_start) * D; for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += blockDim.x) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? v_ptr[local_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Each warp handles 8 keys again float warp_sum = 0.f; for (int jj = 0; jj < keys_per_warp; ++jj) { int j = wid * keys_per_warp + jj; // load score float p = expf(smem_score[j] - m_new); // accumulate sum (only one thread per key) if (lane == 0) { warp_sum += p; } // load V elements half v_reg[4]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { v_reg[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * 4 + d]; } #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { o_reg[d] += p * __half2float(v_reg[d]); } } // accumulate warp_sum across warp to get exact l? That takes 128 reads. 16 threads active. Other threads idle. That's fine; the critical path is short. Similarly, we can have a few threads compute the sum and output. Because for each key `j`, all warps loaded that key and computed dot products for their queries. They wrote scores to `smem_score[j][q]`. So all keys are covered. Bank conflicts: all warps reading the same key `j` simultaneously. `v_j` elements are at `j*D + ...`. For a given `j`, threads with different `lane` read different offsets (`lane*4`). So Phase 2: ```cpp #pragma unroll (maybe not unroll 128) for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { float p0 = expf(smem_score[j][q_local_0] - m_new_0); float p1 = expf(smem_score[j][q_local_1] - m_new_1); l0 += p0; l1 += p1; half v_reg[4]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { v_reg[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * 4 + d]; } #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) { float vf = __half2float(v_reg[d]); o0[d] += p0 * vf; o1[d] += p1 * vf; } } ``` Wait, `l0 += p0` is done by all threads in the warp. But we only need it once per query. Having all 32 threads add `p0` would make `l0` 32x too large! We need only one thread to accumulate `l`. Or we can have each thread compute it but divide later. Better: have lane 0 accumulate `l`. ```cpp if (lane == 0) { l0 += p0; l1 += p1; } ``` Then after the loop, `l0` and `l1` are correct in lane 0. But all threads need `inv_l` for normalization. We can broadcast from lane 0: ```cpp float inv_l0, inv_l1; // after loop if (lane == 0) { // l0 and l1 are final } inv_l0 = __shfl_sync(0xffffffff, 1.0f / l0, 0); inv_l1 = __shfl_sync(0xffffffff, 1.0f / l1, 0); ``` However, we need `l0` and `l1` to be the running sum across tiles, not just the current tile. In the code above, `l0` is updated with `l0 += p0` inside the loop. Since only lane 0 updates it, after the loop `l0` holds the sum for the current tile. But we previously scaled `l0` by `factor` before the loop. So `l0` is correct after the tile. Let's re-evaluate: In online softmax, `l_new = l_old * exp(m_old - m_new) + sum_tile(exp(s - m_new))`. We scaled `l0` by `factor` before the loop. Then in the loop, we add `p0` (which is `exp(s - m_new)`) for each key. So after the loop, `l0 = l_new`. Correct. Similarly for `o0`. This is great. Then after all KV tiles, `l0` is the final sum. Now, we just need to make sure `smem_score` is defined correctly. Let's recapitulate the full kernel with Br=16, Bc=128, NUM_THREADS=256. ```cpp template <int Br=16, int Bc=128, int D=128, int NUM_THREADS=256> __global__ void causal_gqa_kernel(...) { constexpr int D_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int q_per_warp = Br / NUM_WARPS; __shared__ half smem_q[Br * D]; __shared__ half smem_kv[Bc * D]; __shared__ float smem_score[Bc][Br]; // Actually, define smem_score as [Bc][Br] for column-major storage per key? // smem_score[j][q] is what we need. // Bc=128, Br=16 -> 128*16*4 = 8KB. // but in C++ static arrays, [Bc][Br] means row-major: Bc rows, Br columns. // Access smem_score[j][q]. For a fixed j, different q are consecutive. // When threads write to same j but different q, they write consecutive addresses -> good. // When threads read same j but different q, they read consecutive addresses -> good. // No bank conflicts. int batch_idx = blockIdx.z; int head_idx = blockIdx.y; int q_tile_idx = blockIdx.x; int tid = threadIdx.x; int wid = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; int kv_head_idx = head_idx / (num_heads / num_kv_heads); int q_seq0 = q_tile_idx * Br + wid * q_per_warp; int q_local0 = wid * q_per_warp; // ... // Load Q tile int q_offset = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_idx) * seq_len) * D; for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int q_local = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local; half val = (global_q < seq_len) ? q[q_offset + global_q * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_q[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Load query registers float q_reg[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { q_reg[qi][d] = __half2float(smem_q[q_local * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]); } } float m[q_per_warp]; float l[q_per_warp]; float o[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { m[qi] = -1e20f; l[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) o[qi][d] = 0.f; } int kv_offset = ((batch_idx * num_kv_heads + kv_head_idx) * seq_len) * D; int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_start = kv_tile * Bc; // Load K tile for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? k[kv_offset + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Compute scores for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { half k_reg[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { k_reg[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } // dot products for each query in warp float dp[q_per_warp]; for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { dp[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { dp[qi] += q_reg[qi][d] * __half2float(k_reg[d]); } #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { dp[qi] += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset); } } if (lane == 0) { for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; smem_score[j][q_local] = dp[qi]; } } } __syncthreads(); // Apply causal mask and find tile max per query __shared__ float smem_qmax[Br]; if (tid < Br) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + tid; float qmax = -1e20f; for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { int global_k = kv_start + j; float s = smem_score[j][tid]; if (global_k > global_q || global_k >= seq_len) { s = -1e20f; smem_score[j][tid] = s; } if (s > qmax) qmax = s; } smem_qmax[tid] = qmax; } __syncthreads(); // Update online softmax float m_new[q_per_warp]; float factor[q_per_warp]; for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float tile_max = smem_qmax[q_local]; m_new[qi] = fmaxf(m[qi], tile_max); factor[qi] = expf(m[qi] - m_new[qi]); m[qi] = m_new[qi]; } // scale output and l for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { l[qi] *= factor[qi]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= factor[qi]; } } // Load V tile for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? v[kv_offset + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate weighted V for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float p = expf(smem_score[j][q_local] - m_new[qi]); if (lane == 0) { l[qi] += p; } // Note: all threads load V for the same key j but different dims half v_reg[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { v_reg[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] += p * __half2float(v_reg[d]); } } } __syncthreads(); // maybe not needed } // After all tiles, normalize and write output for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local0 + qi; if (global_q >= seq_len) continue; float inv_l = 1.0f / l[qi]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= inv_l; } // write half* out_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch_idx * num_heads + head_idx) * seq_len + global_q) * D; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { out_ptr[lane * D_PER_THREAD + d] = __float2half(o[qi][d]); } } } ``` This kernel looks correct and manageable. **Potential Performance Issues:** 1. The loop over `j=0..Bc-1` in Phase 1 is sequential across keys. For each key, all 256 threads load the same key from SMEM (broadcast) and compute dot products for their queries. Since SMEM broadcast is fast, this is okay. But there are 128 iterations. Each iteration does a warp shuffle. Shuffle latency is a few cycles. The loop might be latency-bound if not unrolled. We can unroll partially. Since Bc=128, we can `#pragma unroll 4` or similar. 2. The max computation: `tid < Br` (16) loops over Bc (128) elements. Very fast. 3. Phase 2 loop: 128 iterations, each reading `smem_score[j][q_local]`. For a fixed `j`, 16 threads (one per query, across warps) read different columns of the same row. Since the row is 16 floats, and there are 32 banks, 16 threads reading consecutive floats map to banks 0..15, no conflict. But there are 8 warps (128 threads) actually reading. Wait, in Phase 2, each warp iterates over all keys for its 2 queries. So for key `j`, all 256 threads read `smem_score[j][q_local]`. 256 threads reading 16 distinct columns (since there are only 16 queries). 16 distinct addresses per key. These addresses map to banks 0..15. Since there are 32 banks, 16 threads from different warps could conflict if they hit the same bank. But each column is 4 bytes. 16 columns * 4 = 64 bytes. 64 bytes / 4 bytes per bank = 16 banks. So exactly banks 0..15. With 256 threads reading 16 addresses, there will be 16-way conflict (16 threads per bank). That's a 16-way bank conflict! Not good. We need to avoid bank conflicts when reading `smem_score[j][q_local]`. Bank conflict analysis: `smem_score[j][q_local]` is row-major: base = `j * Br + q_local`. For fixed `j`, consecutive `q_local` are consecutive words. 16 words = 64 bytes. Bank = (addr / 4) % 32. So consecutive q_local map to banks 0,1,2,...15. Since there are 16 queries, only 16 banks used. If 256 threads read these 16 addresses simultaneously (16 threads per address), we have 16-way conflict. Very bad. Solution: Store `smem_score` with padding to avoid conflicts. Or store transposed: `smem_score[q_local][j]`. Then for fixed `j`, different `q_local` access `q_local * (Bc + pad) + j`. Since `j` is the same, the addresses differ by `(Bc + pad) * 4` bytes. If we choose `Bc + pad` to be odd, they map to different banks. For example, `Bc = 128`. If we pad to 129, then stride = 129 * 4 = 516 bytes. 516 / 4 = 129 words. 129 % 32 = 1. So consecutive rows map to banks +1 mod 32. No conflict! Great. So define `__shared__ float smem_score[Br][Bc + 1];` (or `Bc + pad`). When accessing `smem_score[q_local][j]`, for fixed `j`, threads with different `q_local` access different banks. But `smem_score` is 16 * 129 * 4 = 8256 bytes ≈ 8.1KB. Slightly more than 8KB, still okay. Alternatively, since we write in Phase 1 as `smem_score[j][q_local]` (if row-major), transposed layout changes indexing. Let's just use a 1D array and compute index manually. ```cpp constexpr int SCORE_PAD = 1; __shared__ float smem_score[Br * (Bc + SCORE_PAD)]; #define SCORE(q, j) smem_score[(q) * (Bc + SCORE_PAD) + (j)] ``` Then `SCORE(q, j)` stores score for query `q`, key `j`. When threads access `SCORE(q_local, j)` for different `q_local` and same `j`: Address = `q_local * (Bc+1) + j`. Since `Bc+1 = 129`, difference between consecutive q is 129 words. 129 mod 32 = 1. So banks are strided by 1. 16 queries use 16 different banks. Since 16 threads access these 16 banks simultaneously, and there are 32 banks, each bank has at most one access. Perfect! No bank conflict. When writing in Phase 1: lane 0 of each warp writes `SCORE(q_local, j)`. Again, 16 threads writing to same `j` but different `q_local` -> no bank conflict. So the transposed layout is perfect. But wait, in Phase 1, the inner loop is over `j`. For each `j`, lane 0 of warp writes `SCORE(q_local, j)`. However, different warps write for different `q_local` but same `j`. As analyzed, no bank conflict. But different warps execute in lockstep? They might not, but SMEM handles requests from all warps. Since the addresses are in different banks, it's parallel. When applying causal mask and finding max, the loop is: ```cpp for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { float s = SCORE(tid, j); // tid < Br (16) } ``` Here, 16 threads read different rows but same column `j`. Again, no bank conflict. Then after `j` loop, `smem_qmax[tid] = qmax`. In Phase 2, the loop is: ```cpp for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { float p = expf(SCORE(q_local, j) - m_new); } ``` Again, no bank conflict. This is excellent. So we should define the score buffer as `Br x (Bc+1)`. Since Br=16, Bc=128, pad=1 -> 16*129*4 = 8256 bytes. Good. **Shared memory size:** - `smem_q`: 16*128*2 = 4096 bytes - `smem_kv`: 128*128*2 = 32768 bytes - `smem_score`: 8256 bytes - `smem_qmax`: 16*4 = 64 bytes Total = 4096 + 32768 + 8256 + 64 = 45184 bytes ≈ 44 KB. Blocks per SM on H100: shared mem per SM = 228 KB. So `228 / 44 ≈ 5` blocks per SM. Plus registers. Let's estimate registers per thread: - `q_reg[2][4]` = 8 floats - `o_reg[2][4]` = 8 floats - `m[2]`, `l[2]`, `m_new[2]`, `factor[2]`, `dp[2]` = 10 floats - `k_reg[4]`, `v_reg[4]` = 8 halfs ≈ negligible or 4 floats if converted. - Loop variables etc. Total ~30-40 floats = 40-50 registers. Well under 255. So occupancy is limited by shared memory: 5 blocks per SM * 256 threads = 1280 threads per SM. Max is 2048. So ~62% occupancy in terms of threads. But each block has 8 warps, so 40 warps per SM. Max warps per SM is 64. So ~62% occupancy. Good enough. With 8192 blocks, total waves = 8192 / (132*5) = 12.4 waves. GPU is well utilized. **Grid sizing:** `gridDim.x = (seq_len + Br - 1) / Br;` `gridDim.y = num_heads;` `gridDim.z = batch;` For seq_len=512, Br=16 -> 32 tiles. Total blocks = 32 * 32 * 4 = 4096. Wait, 32 * 32 * 4 = 4096 blocks. Not 8192. Oops. `Br=16`, `seq_len=512`, tiles=32. `num_heads=32`, `batch=4`. 32*32*4=4096 blocks. 4096 blocks / (132*5=660 concurrent) ≈ 6.2 waves. Still good. **Performance estimate:** - Each block does 16 queries * 128 keys * 2 passes * 128 FMAs = 524k FMAs. - 4096 blocks -> 2.1 billion FMAs. - At 134 TFLOPS non-TC, ~16 microseconds. - Memory: loads K and V once per query tile (16 queries share it). Let's finalize the kernel code. **Global memory indexing:** We need to calculate offsets carefully using `int64_t` to avoid overflow for larger batch/seq. But for our sizes, `int` is fine. Still, use `int64_t` for safety. **The actual CUDA code:** ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #define WARP_SIZE 32 #define NEG_INF -1e20f template <int Br, int Bc, int D, int NUM_THREADS> __global__ void causal_gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { constexpr int D_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; // 4 constexpr int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int q_per_warp = Br / NUM_WARPS; __shared__ half smem_q[Br * D]; __shared__ half smem_kv[Bc * D]; __shared__ float smem_qmax[Br]; // padded to avoid bank conflicts constexpr int SCORE_PAD = 1; __shared__ float smem_score[Br * (Bc + SCORE_PAD)]; auto SCORE = [&](int q_id, int k_id) -> float& { return smem_score[q_id * (Bc + SCORE_PAD) + k_id]; }; const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int wid = tid / WARP_SIZE; const int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; const int batch_idx = blockIdx.z; const int head_idx = blockIdx.y; const int q_tile_idx = blockIdx.x; const int kv_head_idx = head_idx / (num_heads / num_kv_heads); const int q_offset_global = ((batch_idx * num_heads + head_idx) * seq_len) * D; const int kv_offset_global = ((batch_idx * num_kv_heads + kv_head_idx) * seq_len) * D; // Load Q tile into SMEM for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int q_local = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local; half val = (global_q < seq_len) ? q[q_offset_global + global_q * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_q[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); const int q_local0 = wid * q_per_warp; // Load query fragments into registers float q_frag[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { q_frag[qi][d] = __half2float(smem_q[q_local * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]); } } float m[q_per_warp]; float l[q_per_warp]; float o[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { m[qi] = NEG_INF; // sentinel l[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] = 0.f; } } const int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_start = kv_tile * Bc; // Load K tile for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? k[kv_offset_global + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Compute scores for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { half k_frag[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { k_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } float dp[q_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { dp[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { dp[qi] += q_frag[qi][d] * __half2float(k_frag[d]); } dp[qi] *= scale; // warp sum #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { dp[qi] += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset); } } if (lane == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; SCORE(q_local, j) = dp[qi]; } } } __syncthreads(); // Apply causal mask and compute per-query tile max if (tid < Br) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + tid; float qmax = NEG_INF; if (global_q < seq_len) { for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { int global_k = kv_start + j; float s = SCORE(tid, j); if (global_k > global_q || global_k >= seq_len) { s = NEG_INF; SCORE(tid, j) = s; } if (s > qmax) qmax = s; } } smem_qmax[tid] = qmax; } __syncthreads(); // Update online softmax stats float m_new[q_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float tile_max = smem_qmax[q_local]; m_new[qi] = fmaxf(m[qi], tile_max); float factor = expf(m[qi] - m_new[qi]); m[qi] = m_new[qi]; l[qi] *= factor; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= factor; } } // Load V tile for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? v[kv_offset_global + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate weighted V sum for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float p = expf(SCORE(q_local, j) - m_new[qi]); if (lane == 0) { l[qi] += p; } half v_frag[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { v_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] += p * __half2float(v_frag[d]); } } } __syncthreads(); } // Write output #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local0 + qi; if (global_q >= seq_len) continue; float inv_l = 1.0f / l[qi]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= inv_l; } half* out_ptr = out + (q_offset_global + global_q * D); #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { out_ptr[lane * D_PER_THREAD + d] = __float2half(o[qi][d]); } } } ``` Wait, in the online softmax, if a query has all keys masked (e.g., invalid query), `m` stays `NEG_INF` and `l` stays 0. Then `inv_l = 1/0` = inf. But we skip writing with `global_q >= seq_len`. What about a query at position 0? It attends to key 0 only. For KV tiles before key 0, all keys are masked. For the tile containing key 0, `tile_max` is the score for key 0. Good. What about query at position 0 and KV tile 0 containing keys 0..127: keys 1..127 are masked. The max is only key 0. `m_new` = score0. `factor` = exp(NEG_INF - m_new) = 0. `l` = 0. Then in Phase 2, for key 0: `p = exp(score0 - m_new) = 1`. `l += 1`. For keys 1..127: `p = exp(NEG_INF - m_new) = 0`. So `l = 1`. `o = v_0`. Output = `v_0`. Correct. Now, what about the very first tile where `m` is `NEG_INF`? `factor = exp(NEG_INF - m_new)`. Since `m_new > NEG_INF` (unless all masked), `NEG_INF - m_new = -inf`, `factor = 0`. So `l` stays 0, `o` stays 0. Good. If all keys in a tile are masked, `tile_max = NEG_INF`. Then `m_new = max(m, NEG_INF) = m`. `factor = 1`. `l` and `o` unchanged. Good. So this algorithm is numerically stable. One thing: `__shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset)` reduces to lane 0 only. So after the loop, only lane 0 holds the sum. In the write step `if (lane == 0) { SCORE(...) = dp[qi]; }`, that's correct. Wait, `__shfl_down_sync` is a down-shift: thread `i` gets value from thread `i+offset`. After offset=16, lane 0 gets from lane 16. After offset=8, lane 0 gets from lane 8 (which now has sum of lane 8..15). After offset=4, lane 0 gets from lane 4 (sum of 4..7). After offset=2, lane 0 gets from lane 2 (sum of 2..3). After offset=1, lane 0 gets from lane 1 (sum of 1..1). So lane 0 ends up with the full sum. Good. Now check shared memory size: `smem_score[Br * (Bc + 1)]`. With `Br=16, Bc=128`: 16*129*4 = 8256 bytes. `smem_q` = 4096 bytes. `smem_kv` = 32768 bytes. `smem_qmax` = 64 bytes. Total = 45184 bytes. With `NUM_THREADS=256`, registers ~50 per thread. Occupancy limited by SMEM: 5 blocks per SM. Could we use `Br=32, Bc=64` to increase tile size and reduce number of blocks? Let's see: `smem_q` = 32*128*2 = 8192. `smem_kv` = 64*128*2 = 16384. `smem_score` = 32*65*4 = 8320. Total = 32896 bytes. Blocks per SM = 6. `seq_len=512`, Br=32 -> 16 tiles. Total blocks = 16*32*4 = 2048. Fewer blocks. But q_per_warp = 32/8 = 4. More registers but okay. With Br=64, Bc=64: `smem_q`=16384, `smem_kv`=16384, `smem_score`=64*65*4=16640. Total=49408. Blocks per SM=4. Total blocks=8*32*4=1024. Even fewer. But KV reads are amortized over 64 queries. However, with only 1024 blocks, we have 1024/528 ≈ 2 waves. Might still be okay. I think Br=16, Bc=128 is a good middle ground. **One more thing: occupancy and shared memory on Hopper.** Hopper max shared memory per block is 227 KB. Our 44KB is fine. But we might want to use dynamic shared memory or let the compiler allocate. We can just use static arrays. **Potential bug: `smem_score` indexing lambda inside kernel.** CUDA lambdas without `__device__` cannot capture in certain contexts, but a plain accessor macro is fine. Let's use a macro or manual indexing. A simple macro: ```cpp #define SCORE(q_id, k_id) smem_score[(q_id) * (Bc + SCORE_PAD) + (k_id)] ``` This works. **Compilation flags:** We should add `-O3`, `--use_fast_math`. Maybe `--expt-relaxed-constexpr` if needed. **Python wrapper:** ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline # We need to ensure the kernel is compiled only once. _cuda_mod = None def get_cuda_module(): global _cuda_mod if _cuda_mod is None: _cuda_mod = load_inline( name="gqa_cuda_hopper", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=["gqa_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode", "arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"], verbose=False, ) return _cuda_mod class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size): super().__init__() self.num_heads = num_heads self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads self.head_size = head_size self.scale = head_size ** -0.5 def forward(self, q, k, v): # q: [B, H, S, D], k/v: [B, Hkv, S, D] # Ensure contiguous, fp16, cuda q = q.contiguous() k = k.contiguous() v = v.contiguous() mod = get_cuda_module() return mod.gqa_forward(q, k, v, self.scale) ``` Wait, `load_inline` compiles the code when called. Should we call it in `__init__` or lazily? Both fine. Lazy is okay. **Checking `gqa_forward` signature:** ```cpp at::Tensor gqa_forward(at::Tensor q, at::Tensor k, at::Tensor v, float scale) { ... } ``` Need to include `ATen/ATen.h` or just `torch/extension.h`. **Type checks:** The kernel assumes `half`. We should check `q.scalar_type() == at::kHalf`. **Launch bounds:** We might add `__launch_bounds__(256)` to the kernel to help the compiler. ```cpp template <int Br, int Bc, int D, int NUM_THREADS> __launch_bounds__(NUM_THREADS) __global__ void causal_gqa_kernel(...) { ... } ``` **Alignment of `half` pointers:** Global memory accesses in the kernel are coalesced if the starting address is aligned. PyTorch tensors are typically aligned to at least 256 bytes. For `half`, `D_PER_THREAD=4`, each thread loads 4 consecutive halfs. That's 8 bytes. With 256 threads, each warp loads 32*8=256 bytes, aligned. So coalesced. **Warp specialization?** Not needed. **TMA?** Not possible with inline CUDA without complex PTX. **Final check of numerical correctness:** - The kernel computes `softmax(QK^T / sqrt(D)) @ V` with causal mask. - Order of operations: dot products in fp32, scaled by `scale`. Max reduces across each row via `fmaxf`. Exponent `expf`. Weighted sum in fp32. Final normalization `1/l`. - The paged attention v1 kernel does essentially the same, but our kernel does it for multiple queries per block. - Differences: The reference paged attention v1 might apply `scale` in a different place (e.g., before or after Q load). But it's multiplicative, so order doesn't matter. - One subtle difference: paged attention v1 computes output in an online manner without explicitly dividing by `l` at the end? Actually, the v1 kernel does online softmax and accumulates output. At the end, it divides by `l`. So same. - Another subtle difference: paged attention v1 might not explicitly compute `exp(s - m_new) * v` within the tile but after the loop? Our method matches the standard online softmax algorithm. - Tolerance: fp16 atol=1e-2. Should pass. **Potential issue with `__shfl_down_sync`:** We used `__shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset)` inside a loop. This is correct. **Potential issue with `smem_score` and warp synchronization:** In Phase 1, after computing scores, lane 0 writes to `smem_score`. Other lanes do nothing. Then `__syncthreads()` ensures all writes are visible. Good. **Potential issue with Phase 2 and `smem_score` reads:** All warps read `smem_score` in the `j` loop. For a fixed `j`, they read `SCORE(q_local, j)` for different `q_local` (0..15). Since we padded to 129 columns, no bank conflict. Good. **Potential issue with first query tile and causal mask:** Query global_q = 0. KV tile 0: keys 0..127. Keys 1..127 masked. Score for key 0 computed. `tile_max` = score0. `m_new` = score0. `factor` = exp(-1e20f - score0) = 0. `l` remains 0. Then in Phase 2, key 0: `p = exp(score0 - score0) = 1`. `l` becomes 1. `o` accumulates `v0`. Keys 1..127: `p = exp(NEG_INF - score0) = 0`. No contribution. Output = `v0`. Correct. **Potential issue with `NEG_INF` and `expf`:** `expf(-1e20f)` underflows to 0.0f. That's fine. `expf(-1e20f - (-1e10f)) = expf(-9.9999e19) = 0`. Fine. **Potential issue with `m_new` for invalid queries:** If `global_q >= seq_len`, `tile_max = NEG_INF`. `m_new = max(NEG_INF, NEG_INF) = NEG_INF`. `factor = exp(0) = 1`? Wait, `m` is initialized to `NEG_INF`. So `m[qi]` = NEG_INF. `m_new` = NEG_INF. `factor = exp(NEG_INF - NEG_INF) = exp(NaN)` = NaN. This would corrupt output. We need to handle invalid queries. Since the test seq_len=512 is multiple of Br=16, there are no invalid queries in the tile. But for safety, let's handle it. Fix: Initialize `m` to `-1e10f` (or any finite value less than real scores). Wait, if we initialize `m` to `-1e10f`, for a real query with all scores > -1e10f, `factor = exp(-1e10f - m_new)` where `m_new` is the tile max. `-1e10f - m_new` is a huge negative number. `factor` = 0. Good. For invalid queries, `tile_max = NEG_INF`. `m_new = max(-1e10f, NEG_INF) = -1e10f`. `factor = exp(-1e10f - (-1e10f)) = 1`. `l` stays 0. Then in Phase 2, all `p = exp(NEG_INF - (-1e10f)) = 0`. So `o` stays 0. At the end, `inv_l = 1/0 = inf`. Output would be NaN if we write. But we skip writing for `global_q >= seq_len`. Good. For a query at position 0: first tile (keys 0..127) has `tile_max = score0`. `m_new = max(-1e10f, score0) = score0`. `factor = exp(-1e10f - score0) = 0`. `l` becomes 0. In Phase 2, key0: `p = exp(score0 - score0) = 1`. `l` becomes 1. `o` = v0. Keys 1..127: p=0. Good. At the end, `inv_l = 1`. Output = v0. Correct. What about subsequent tiles where all keys are masked? `tile_max = NEG_INF`. `m_new = max(score0, NEG_INF) = score0`. `factor = exp(score0 - score0) = 1`. `l` unchanged. `o` unchanged. Good. What if a tile has some valid keys and some masked, and the valid max is less than previous `m`? `m_new = m`. `factor = 1`. `l` and `o` unchanged. We add contributions from valid keys with probabilities `exp(s - m)`. This is correct for online softmax. So initializing `m = -1e10f` (a sentinel lower than any real score) works perfectly. Let's use `INIT_M = -1e10f`. What is the minimum possible score? `scale = 1/sqrt(128) ≈ 0.088`. `q` and `k` are standard normal ~ N(0,1). So `q·k` is sum of 128 products of normals. Mean 0, variance 128. So typical magnitude is sqrt(128) ≈ 11.3. Scaled by 0.088 gives ~1.0. So `-1e10f` is way below. Good. Let's change initialization: ```cpp float m[q_per_warp]; float l[q_per_warp]; ... m[qi] = -1e10f; ``` In the max-finding step, for invalid queries we can set `smem_qmax[tid] = -1e10f` or just leave it as the loop doesn't run. Actually, the loop over `j` only runs if `global_q < seq_len`. If not, `qmax` stays `NEG_INF`. Then `m_new = max(-1e10f, NEG_INF) = -1e10f`. Then Phase 2 runs with all scores = NEG_INF. `p = exp(NEG_INF - (-1e10f)) = 0`. `l` stays 0. Output write skipped. Good. But note: `smem_qmax` is only written by `tid < Br`. For invalid queries (if any), `qmax` is uninitialized if we don't set it. So we must initialize `qmax` to `-1e10f` (or `NEG_INF`) for all `tid < Br` regardless. Let's set `qmax = -1e10f` by default. For invalid queries, it remains `-1e10f`. But the `if (global_q < seq_len)` condition currently wraps the loop. We should set `qmax = NEG_INF` when `global_q >= seq_len`. Wait, if `qmax = NEG_INF`, then `m_new = max(-1e10f, NEG_INF) = -1e10f`. Same as if `qmax = -1e10f`. So either is fine. But to be safe, let's not use `NEG_INF` for `qmax` of invalid queries, use `-1e10f`. Actually, it's simplest to remove the `if (global_q < seq_len)` guard in the max loop and instead incorporate it into the mask condition: ```cpp for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { int global_k = kv_start + j; float s = SCORE(tid, j); if (global_k > global_q || global_k >= seq_len || global_q >= seq_len) { s = NEG_INF; SCORE(tid, j) = s; } if (s > qmax) qmax = s; } ``` This is cleaner. Let's integrate all these. One final point: The provided source code uses `vllm::paged_attention_v1_kernel` which has a `scale` parameter. Our `gqa_forward` wrapper also takes scale and passes it. **Let's write the final code.** I will produce a single code block with the Python class `ModelNew` and the inline CUDA code. But for our problem sizes, `int` is fine. However, to be safe, cast to `int64_t` for the base offset. The problem says head_size=128. Templating allows compile-time unrolling. But it means we compile only for D=128. That's fine. In the wrapper, we can assert `head_size == 128`. **pybind11 or torch::Library?** Using `PYBIND11_MODULE` in `cuda_sources` is standard. Let's do that. **Code:** ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn import math from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline _CUDA_MODULE = None def _get_cuda_module(): global _CUDA_MODULE if _CUDA_MODULE is None: _CUDA_MODULE = load_inline( name="gqa_hopper", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #define WARP_SIZE 32 #define NEG_INF -1e20f #define INIT_M -1e10f template <int Br, int Bc, int D, int NUM_THREADS> __launch_bounds__(NUM_THREADS) __global__ void causal_gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { constexpr int D_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; } } float m[q_per_warp]; float l[q_per_warp]; float o[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { m[qi] = INIT_M; l[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; } __syncthreads(); // Compute scores for this tile #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { half k_frag[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { k_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } float dp[q_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { dp[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { dp[qi] += q_frag[qi][d] * __half2float(k_frag[d]); } dp[qi] *= scale; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { dp[qi] += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset); } } if (lane == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; SCORE(q_local, j) = dp[qi]; } } } __syncthreads(); // Causal mask + tile max per query if (tid < Br) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + tid; float qmax = NEG_INF; #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { int global_k = kv_start + j; float s = SCORE(tid, j); if (global_k > global_q || global_k >= seq_len) { s = NEG_INF; SCORE(tid, j) = s; } if (s > qmax) qmax = s; } smem_qmax[tid] = qmax; } __syncthreads(); // Update softmax state float m_new[q_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float tile_max = smem_qmax[q_local]; m_new[qi] = fmaxf(m[qi], tile_max); float factor = expf(m[qi] - m_new[qi]); m[qi] = m_new[qi]; l[qi] *= factor; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= factor; } } // Load V tile (reuse smem_kv) for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? v[kv_offset_global + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate output #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float p = expf(SCORE(q_local, j) - m_new[qi]); if (lane == 0) { l[qi] += p; } half v_frag[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { v_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] += p * __half2float(v_frag[d]); } } } __syncthreads(); } // Write output #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local0 + qi; if (global_q >= seq_len) continue; float inv_l = 1.0f / l[qi]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= inv_l; } half* out_ptr = out + (q_offset_global + global_q * D); #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { out_ptr[lane * D_PER_THREAD + d] = __float2half(o[qi][d]); } } #undef SCORE } // Wrapper at::Tensor gqa_forward(at::Tensor q, at::Tensor k, at::Tensor v, float scale) { TORCH_CHECK(q.is_cuda(), "q must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(k.is_cuda(), "k must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(v.is_cuda(), "v must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(q.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "q must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(k.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "k must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(v.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "v must be fp16"); int batch = q.size(0); int num_heads = q.size(1); int seq_len = q.size(2); int head_size = q.size(3); int num_kv_heads = k.size(1); auto out = torch::empty_like(q); constexpr int Br = 16; constexpr int Bc = 128; constexpr int D = 128; constexpr int NUM_THREADS = 256; TORCH_CHECK(head_size == D, "head_size must be ", D); dim3 grid((seq_len + Br - 1) / Br, num_heads, batch); dim3 block(NUM_THREADS); causal_gqa_kernel<Br, Bc, D, NUM_THREADS><<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<half*>(q.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(k.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(v.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr()), batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); return out; } PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("gqa_forward", &gqa_forward, "Causal GQA forward"); } """, functions=["gqa_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=[ "-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode", "arch=compute_90,code=sm_90", ], verbose=False, ) return _CUDA_MODULE class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size): super().__init__() self.num_heads = num_heads self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads self.head_size = head_size self.scale = float(head_size) ** -0.5 def forward(self, q, k, v): # Ensure contiguous memory layout q = q.contiguous() k = k.contiguous() v = v.contiguous() mod = _get_cuda_module() return mod.gqa_forward(q, k, v, self.scale) ``` Wait, `load_inline` `functions` argument? The signature of `load_inline` in PyTorch: `load_inline(name, cpp_sources, cuda_sources, functions, extra_cuda_cflags, verbose, ...)`. The `functions` list tells it which functions to expose from the pybind module. It compiles a `pybind11` module under the hood. The `PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m)` is required. So providing `functions=["gqa_forward"]` is correct. But actually, `pybind11` is used automatically by `load_inline` if you put the binding code in `cpp_sources` or `cuda_sources`. Let's verify: In PyTorch, `load_inline` usually works by generating a small wrapper around the provided code. If you include `PYBIND11_MODULE` yourself, it should compile. `functions` is just a hint for PyTorch to know what's available, but it's not strictly necessary if the module is self-contained. However, passing `functions` is standard. Wait, the `load_inline` documentation says: `functions` is a list of function names that should be accessible from Python. It expects the C++ code to define these functions and bind them via pybind11 or torchbind. When `cuda_sources` contains the `PYBIND11_MODULE`, it should work. I think it's fine. One potential issue: `PYBIND11_MODULE` inside `cuda_sources`. Some versions of PyTorch's `load_inline` might not handle pybind inside `cuda_sources` properly. To be safe, we can put the kernel declaration in `cuda_sources` and the wrapper/binding in `cpp_sources`. Let's split: - `cpp_sources`: Forward declaration of the kernel, the `gqa_forward` function, and `PYBIND11_MODULE`. - `cuda_sources`: The actual kernel. But forward declaration of a templated kernel can be tricky across translation units. `cpp_sources` is compiled with `g++`, `cuda_sources` with `nvcc`. We need to use `extern "C"` or declare the kernel in the `.cpp` and define it in `.cu`. For templates, we can explicitly instantiate. Simpler: Put everything in `cuda_sources`. `nvcc` can compile host code with `pybind11`. I believe it works. Many online examples do this. However, if it doesn't compile, we have no chance to fix. Let's see if there's any C++ issue. `torch/extension.h` includes `pybind11`. `nvcc` supports `pybind11` headers. Should be fine. Another approach: Use `torch::Library` to register a custom op. That avoids `pybind11` in `.cu`. Example: ```cpp TORCH_LIBRARY_FRAGMENT(gqa_hopper, m) { m.def("gqa_forward(Tensor q, Tensor k, Tensor v, float scale) -> Tensor"); } TORCH_LIBRARY_IMPL(gqa_hopper, CUDA, m) { m.impl("gqa_forward", [](at::Tensor q, at::Tensor k, at::Tensor v, float scale) -> at::Tensor { ... }); } ``` This is pure torch library and avoids pybind. But `TORCH_LIBRARY` might have issues with `load_inline`. Actually, `load_inline` can handle it? Not sure. Actually, `load_inline` with `cpp_sources` and `cuda_sources` compiles them into a shared library. If we use `TORCH_LIBRARY`, the library registers the ops upon loading. We can then call `torch.ops.gqa_hopper.gqa_forward`. That should work. But using `PYBIND11_MODULE` and `m.def` is simpler and more direct. Let's stick with that. Wait, I recall that in `load_inline`, the `functions` argument is used to search for functions in the compiled library. It expects plain C functions or pybind11 functions. If we define `PYBIND11_MODULE`, the module is named `TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME`. `load_inline` handles the rest. Let's verify the `load_inline` signature: `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline(name, cpp_sources, cuda_sources=None, functions=None, extra_cflags=None, extra_cuda_cflags=None, extra_ldflags=None, extra_include_paths=None, build_directory=None, verbose=False, with_cuda=None, is_python_module=True, keep_intermediates=True)` Yes. `is_python_module=True` (default) means it expects a Python extension module. `PYBIND11_MODULE` is appropriate. I'll output the code as a single Python block. One more check: `q.permute(0,2,1,3)`? No, the input is already `[B, H, S, D]`. The standard is `[B, H, S, D]`. Our kernel uses this order. But wait, in many attention implementations, the layout is `[B, S, H, D]`. The I/O contract explicitly says: ```python q = torch.randn(BATCH, NUM_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE, ...) ``` So it is `[B, H, S, D]`. Good. One more check: `k` and `v` have `NUM_KV_HEADS`. Our kernel accesses them as `k[batch, kv_head, seq, d]`. The layout matches. **Potential performance improvement:** Since the loop over `j` in Phase 1 and Phase 2 is not fully unrolled, the compiler might not unroll it. With `Bc=128`, `#pragma unroll 4` is okay. Also, inside the inner loop, we load `k_frag` each iteration. The compiler might keep it in registers. **Potential numerical issue:** In `max-finding` step, we set `smem_qmax[tid] = qmax` where `qmax` could be `NEG_INF` if all masked. Then `m_new = fmaxf(-1e10f, -1e20f) = -1e10f`. `factor = exp(-1e10f - (-1e10f)) = 1`. In Phase 2, `exp(SCORE - m_new)` where SCORE = NEG_INF = -1e20f. `exp(-1e20f - (-1e10f)) = exp(-1e20f) = 0`. Good. `l` unchanged. After all tiles, for a completely masked query (e.g., invalid), `l=0`, `inv_l=inf`, output = NaN. But we skip writing for `global_q >= seq_len`. For a valid query at position 0, the first few tiles might be all masked? No, tile 0 contains key 0, so not all masked. What about `seq_len` not being a multiple of `Bc`? The last tile has some invalid keys. They are masked. Fine. **Compilation flags:** I added `-gencode arch=compute_90,code=sm_90` to target Hopper. This is important. H100 is sm_90. The default might be lower. Adding this ensures Hopper instructions are used. **One subtle issue:** `__shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset)` inside a template kernel with variable `q_per_warp`. The compiler should unroll the loops and generate valid code. **Final check on memory indexing:** `smem_q[q_local * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]` where `q_local` is 0..15, `lane` is 0..31, `d` is 0..3. Max index: 15*128 + 31*4 + 3 = 1920 + 124 + 3 = 2047. `smem_q` size is `Br*D = 16*128 = 2048` (halfs). Index 2047 is within bounds. Good. `smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]` where `j` is 0..127. Max index: 127*128 + 31*4 + 3 = 16256 + 127 = 16383. `smem_kv` size is `Bc*D = 128*128 = 16384`. Good. `SCORE(q_local, j)`: `q_local` max 15, `j` max 127. `15 * 129 + 127 = 1935 + 127 = 2062`. Array size `16*129=2064`. Good. **One concern: `load_inline` caching:** The module is cached by name. Using a fixed name `gqa_hopper` means if we change code, it might not recompile unless the build directory is cleared. assistant```python import torch import torch.nn as nn import math from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline _CUDA_MODULE = None def _get_cuda_module(): global _CUDA_MODULE if _CUDA_MODULE is None: _CUDA_MODULE = load_inline( name="gqa_hopper_optimized", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #define WARP_SIZE 32 #define NEG_INF -1e20f #define INIT_M -1e10f template <int Br, int Bc, int D, int NUM_THREADS> __launch_bounds__(NUM_THREADS) __global__ void causal_gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { constexpr int D_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE; constexpr int q_per_warp = Br / NUM_WARPS; __shared__ half smem_q[Br * D]; __shared__ half smem_kv[Bc * D]; __shared__ float smem_qmax[Br]; constexpr int SCORE_PAD = 1; __shared__ float smem_score[Br * (Bc + SCORE_PAD)]; #define SCORE(q_id, k_id) smem_score[(q_id) * (Bc + SCORE_PAD) + (k_id)] const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int wid = tid / WARP_SIZE; const int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; const int batch_idx = blockIdx.z; const int head_idx = blockIdx.y; const int q_tile_idx = blockIdx.x; const int kv_head_idx = head_idx / (num_heads / num_kv_heads); const int64_t q_offset_global = ((int64_t)batch_idx * num_heads + head_idx) * seq_len * D; const int64_t kv_offset_global = ((int64_t)batch_idx * num_kv_heads + kv_head_idx) * seq_len * D; /* Load Q tile */ for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int q_local = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local; half val = (global_q < seq_len) ? q[q_offset_global + global_q * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_q[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); const int q_local0 = wid * q_per_warp; /* Load query fragments into registers */ float q_frag[q_per_warp][D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { m[qi] = INIT_M; l[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] = 0.f; } } const int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; /* Iterate over KV sequence tiles */ for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_start = kv_tile * Bc; /* Load K tile */ for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? k[kv_offset_global + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); /* Phase 1: compute score tile */ #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { half k_frag[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { k_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } float dp[q_per_warp]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { dp[qi] = 0.f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { dp[qi] += q_frag[qi][d] * __half2float(k_frag[d]); } dp[qi] *= scale; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { dp[qi] += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset); } } if (lane == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; SCORE(q_local, j) = dp[qi]; } } } __syncthreads(); /* Apply causal mask and compute per-query tile max */ if (tid < Br) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + tid; float qmax = NEG_INF; #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { int global_k = kv_start + j; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float tile_max = smem_qmax[q_local]; m_new[qi] = fmaxf(m[qi], tile_max); float factor = expf(m[qi] - m_new[qi]); m[qi] = m_new[qi]; l[qi] *= factor; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= factor; } } /* Load V tile (reuse smem_kv) */ for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? v[kv_offset_global + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); /* Phase 2: accumulate weighted V */ #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float p = expf(SCORE(q_local, j) - m_new[qi]); if (lane == 0) { l[qi] += p; } half v_frag[D_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { v_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] += p * __half2float(v_frag[d]);
