#pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= inv_l; } half* out_ptr = out + (q_offset_global + global_q * D); #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { out_ptr[lane * D_PER_THREAD + d] = __float2half(o[qi][d]); } } #undef SCORE } /* Host launcher */ at::Tensor gqa_forward(at::Tensor q, at::Tensor k, at::Tensor v, float scale) { TORCH_CHECK(q.is_cuda(), "q must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(k.is_cuda(), "k must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(v.is_cuda(), "v must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(q.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "q must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(k.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "k must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(v.scalar_type() == at::kHalf, "v must be fp16"); int batch = q.size(0); int num_heads = q.size(1); int seq_len = q.size(2); int head_size = q.size(3); int num_kv_heads = k.size(1); auto out = torch::empty_like(q); constexpr int Br = 16; constexpr int Bc = 128; constexpr int D = 128; constexpr int NUM_THREADS = 256; TORCH_CHECK(head_size == D, "Unsupported head size"); dim3 grid((seq_len + Br - 1) / Br, num_heads, batch); dim3 block(NUM_THREADS); causal_gqa_kernel<Br, Bc, D, NUM_THREADS><<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<half*>(q.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(k.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(v.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr()), batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale); Let me also make the kernel simpler: no templating, just `#define` constants. Fixed Br=32, Bc=128, D=128, NUM_THREADS=256. Kernel design: - Grid: `(seq_len / Br, num_heads, batch)` - Block: 256 threads Inside kernel: ```cpp __global__ void causal_gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int batch, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { const int Br = 32; const int Bc = 128; const int D = 128; const int NUM_THREADS = 256; const int NUM_WARPS = NUM_THREADS / WARP_SIZE; // 8 const int q_per_warp = Br / NUM_WARPS; // 4 const int D_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; // 4 __shared__ half smem_q[Br * D]; __shared__ half smem_kv[Bc * D]; __shared__ float smem_qmax[Br]; __shared__ float smem_score[Br * (Bc + 1)]; int tid = threadIdx.x; int wid = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; int batch_idx = blockIdx.z; int head_idx = blockIdx.y; int q_tile_idx = blockIdx.x; int kv_head_idx = head_idx / (num_heads / num_kv_heads); int64_t q_offset = ((int64_t)batch_idx * num_heads + head_idx) * seq_len * D; int64_t kv_offset = ((int64_t)batch_idx * num_kv_heads + kv_head_idx) * seq_len * D; // Load Q tile for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int q_local = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local; half val = (global_q < seq_len) ? q[q_offset + global_q * D + d] : __float2half(0.0f); smem_q[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); int q_local0 = wid * q_per_warp; // Load Q fragment to registers float q_frag[4][4]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { q_frag[qi][d] = __half2float(smem_q[q_local * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]); } } float m[4], l[4], o[4][4]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { m[qi] = -1e10f; l[qi] = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] = 0.0f; } } int num_kv_tiles = (seq_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_start = kv_tile * Bc; // Load K tile for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? k[kv_offset + global_k * D + d] : __float2half(0.0f); smem_kv[i] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Compute scores #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { half k_frag[4]; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { k_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d]; } float dp[4]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { dp[qi] = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { dp[qi] += q_frag[qi][d] * __half2float(k_frag[d]); } dp[qi] *= scale; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { dp[qi] += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp[qi], offset); } } if (lane == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; smem_score[q_local * (Bc + 1) + j] = dp[qi]; } } } __syncthreads(); // Apply causal mask and compute max if (tid < Br) { int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + tid; float qmax = -1e20f; #pragma unroll 4 for (int j = 0; j < Bc; ++j) { int global_k = kv_start + j; float s = smem_score[tid * (Bc + 1) + j]; if (global_k > global_q || global_k >= seq_len) { s = -1e20f; smem_score[tid * (Bc + 1) + j] = s; } if (s > qmax) qmax = s; } smem_qmax[tid] = qmax; } __syncthreads(); // Update online softmax float m_new[4]; #pragma unroll for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) { int q_local = q_local0 + qi; float tile_max = smem_qmax[q_local]; m_new[qi] = fmaxf(m[qi], tile_max); float factor = expf(m[qi] - m_new[qi]); m[qi] = m_new[qi]; l[qi] *= factor; #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) { o[qi][d] *= factor; } } // Load V tile for (int i = tid; i < Bc * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int local_k = i / D; int d = i % D; int global_k = kv_start + local_k; half val = (global_k < seq_len) ? Shared memory size: 32*128*2 + 128*128*2 + 32*129*4 + 32*4 = 8192 + 32768 + 16512 + 128 = ~57600 bytes. < 164KB (A100) even. So certainly fine for H100. Let me write the full Python module now. j < Bc; ++j) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) {\n int q_local = q_local0 + qi;\n float p = expf(smem_score[q_local * (Bc + 1) + j] - m_new[qi]);\n if (lane == 0) {\n l[qi] += p;\n }\n half v_frag[4];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) {\n v_frag[d] = smem_kv[j * D + lane * D_PER_THREAD + d];\n }\n #pragma unroll\n for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) {\n o[qi][d] += p * __half2float(v_frag[d]);\n }\n }\n }\n __syncthreads();\n }\n\n // Normalize and write output\n #pragma unroll\n for (int qi = 0; qi < q_per_warp; ++qi) {\n int global_q = q_tile_idx * Br + q_local0 + qi;\n if (global_q >= seq_len) continue;\n float inv_l = 1.0f / l[qi];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) {\n o[qi][d] *= inv_l;\n }\n half* out_ptr = out + (q_offset + global_q * D);\n #pragma unroll\n for (int d = 0; d < D_PER_THREAD; ++d) {\n out_ptr[lane * D_PER_THREAD + d] = __float2half(o[qi][d]);\n }\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor gqa_forward_cuda(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v, float scale) {\n int batch = q.size(0);\n int num_heads = q.size(1);\n int seq_len = q.size(2);\n int head_size = q.size(3);\n int num_kv_heads = k.size(1);\n\n auto out = torch::empty_like(q);\n\n const int Br = 32;\n const int Bc = 128;\n const int NUM_THREADS = 256;\n\n dim3 grid((seq_len + Br - 1) / Br, num_heads, batch);\n dim3 block(NUM_THREADS);\n\n causal_gqa_kernel<<<grid, block>>>(\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(k.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(v.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n batch, num_heads, num_kv_heads, seq_len, scale);\n\n return out;\n}\n\"\"\"\n\n _CUDA_MODULE = load_inline(\n name=\"gqa_hopper_v3\",\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=cuda_source,\n functions=[\"gqa_forward\"],\n extra_cflags=[\"-O3\"],\n extra_cuda_cflags=[\"-O3\", \"--use_fast_math\"],\n verbose=False,\n )\n return _CUDA_MODULE\n\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size):\n super().__init__()\n self.num_heads = num_heads\n self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads\n self.head_size = head_size\n self.scale = float(head_size) ** -0.5\n\n def forward(self, q, k, v):\n q = q.contiguous()\n k = k.contiguous()\n v = v.contiguous()\n mod = _get_cuda_module()\n return mod.gqa_forward(q, k, v, self.scale)\n" }

Let me write this simpler kernel: ```cpp __global__ void gqa_kernel( const half* q, const half* k, const half* v, half* out, int B, int H, int Hkv, int S, int D, float scale) { int b = blockIdx.z; int h = blockIdx.y; int q_pos = blockIdx.x; int kv_h = h / (H / Hkv); int tid = threadIdx.x; // Each thread handles one dimension int d = tid; int64_t q_base = ((int64_t)b * H + h) * S * D + q_pos * D; int64_t kv_base = ((int64_t)b * Hkv + kv_h) * S * D; float q_val = __half2float(q[q_base + d]); float m = -1e10f; float l = 0.0f; float o = 0.0f; // Process KV in tiles const int Bc = 128; // or 64 depending on shared mem __shared__ half smem_k[Bc * D]; n __shared__ float smem_score[Bc]; int num_tiles = (q_pos + 1 + Bc - 1) / Bc; // only up to q_pos for causal for (int tile = 0; tile < num_tiles; ++tile) { int kv_start = tile * Bc; int kv_end = min(kv_start + Bc, q_pos + 1); int tile_len = kv_end - kv_start; // Load K tile cooperatively // Each of the 128 threads loads Bc elements? // Bc * D = Bc * 128. With 128 threads, each loads Bc elements. for (int i = 0; i < Bc; ++i) { int idx = tid * Bc + i; int local_pos = idx / D; int local_d = idx % D; // This doesn't distribute well if Bc != D } } } ``` Actually, loading a tile of size Bc x D with D threads: - Total elements = Bc * D. With D threads, each thread loads Bc elements. - Thread d loads elements: (0, d), (1, d), ..., (Bc-1, d) - But with coalescing, thread d loads at addresses: kv_base + (kv_start + j) * D + d - This is perfectly coalesced! All threads access contiguous addresses. Similarly for V. For computing dot products: - Thread d has q[d] and k[j][d] for each j in the tile. - Thread d computes sum_j q[d] * k[j][d]? No, we need to sum across d, not j. - Actually, for each KV position j, score[j] = sum_d q[d] * k[j][d] - Thread d can compute partial products q[d] * k[j][d] for all j and store in a register - Then we need to reduce across all dimensions d for each j If we store score[j] locally for each thread, we need Bc floats per thread. With Bc=32, that's 32 floats per thread = manageable. But with Bc=128, that's 512 bytes per thread. With 128 threads per block, we might exceed registers. Alternative: compute dot product for one KV position at a time. - Load one K row into registers (or shared mem broadcast) - Each thread computes q[d] * k[d] - Warp shuffle reduce - Store score in shared memory - Repeat for all Bc positions With D=128 and block size 128: - Each thread has q_val - Load K row j: each thread loads k_val = smem_k[j * D + d] (or directly from memory for the first time) - Compute q_val * k_val - Warp shuffle reduce across 128 threads... but warp shuffle is only within a warp of 32. With 4 warps, we need to either: a) Use shared memory reduction across warps b) Use cooperative groups reduce Actually D=128 and 128 threads = 4 warps. To reduce 128 partial sums to 1, we can use shared memory: ```cpp __shared__ float warp_sums[4]; // Each warp reduces to lane 0 float dp = q_val * k_val; for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) dp += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp, offset); if (lane == 0) warp_sums[warp_id] = dp; __syncthreads(); // Thread 0 sums the 4 warps if (tid == 0) { float total = warp_sums[0] + warp_sums[1] + warp_sums[2] + warp_sums[3]; score[j] = total * scale; } __syncthreads(); ``` This is clean and simple. Let me write the full kernel this way. For the V accumulation: - For each KV position j, we have score p[j] = exp(score[j] - m_new) - We need o[d] += p[j] * v[j][d] for each d - Each thread holds o[d] and loads v[j][d] to compute update - Perfect parallelization across D. So the kernel for a single block (1 query position): ```cpp __shared__ half smem_k[Bc * D]; // But Bc*128 might be large __shared__ half smem_v[Bc * D]; __shared__ float smem_score[Bc]; ``` Wait, can we fit K and V simultaneously? - Bc=64, D=128: 64*128 = 8192 halfs = 16KB per array - K tile + V tile = 32KB - Score array = 64 * 4 bytes = 256 bytes - Total ~33KB. Very comfortable, and we can even increase Bc. If Bc=128: - K + V = 64KB - Still very comfortable. Actually, the simplest is to not even use shared memory for K and V, but load from global memory directly with good coalescing. Since we're memory bandwidth bound anyway and the cache on H100 is huge (50MB L2), a seq_len of 512 might even fit in L2. For causal attention, each query position only needs to access positions 0..q_pos. The total K and V data accessed across all query positions is: - For K: sum over q_pos of (q_pos * D * sizeof(half)) = ~S^2/2 * D * 2 bytes = 512*512/2 * 128 * 2 = 33.5 MB per head - For batch=4, num_heads=32: total = 4 * 32 * 33.5 MB = 4.3 GB Actually wait, since K is shared among 4 query heads (GQA), for 8 KV heads: - Total K accesses = 4 * 8 * 33.5 MB = 1.07 GB Hmm, this is bandwidth of 1.07 GB per forward. At 3350 GB/s, that's 0.3 ms in theory. But with other overhead, maybe 0.5-1 ms. For compute, the matmul is: - Q@K^T: batch * num_heads * S * S * D / 2 = 4 * 32 * 512 * 512 * 128 / 2 = 2.1 BFLOPS - P@V: batch * num_heads * S * S * D / 2 (but with GQA, P uses shared KV) = 4 * 32 * 512 * 512 * 128 / 2 / 4 = 0.53 BFLOPS - Total ~2.6 BFLOPS At 2000 TFLOPS, compute time is ~1.3 microseconds. So clearly this is memory bandwidth bound even with FlashAttention-style tiling. For a bandwidth-bound kernel on H100, the most important thing is to coalesce memory accesses and avoid unnecessary loads/stores. Given that each query position only sees a triangular amount of K/V, a simple per-query block kernel might actually do well because: 1. No wasted computation on masked positions 2. K and V are loaded once per query position and fully utilized 3. No need for complex shared memory management Let me write a straightforward kernel where: - Grid: [seq_len, num_heads, batch] - Block: 128 threads - Each block computes one (batch, head, q_pos) output - Threads cooperate to load K[0:q_pos+1] and V[0:q_pos+1] in chunks - Compute dot products, softmax, and output ```cpp __global__ void gqa_kernel(...) { int b = blockIdx.z; int h = blockIdx.y; int q_pos = blockIdx.x; int tid = threadIdx.x; int kv_h = h / (H / Hkv); int64_t q_base = ((int64_t)b * H + h) * S * D; int64_t kv_base = ((int64_t)b * Hkv + kv_h) * S * D; float q_val = __half2float(q[q_base + q_pos * D + tid]); float m = -1e10f; float l = 0.0f; float o = 0.0f; const int Bc = 64; // chunk size __shared__ half smem_k[Bc * 128]; __shared__ half smem_v[Bc * 128]; __shared__ float smem_score[Bc]; __shared__ float warp_sums[4]; // 4 warps int kv_len = q_pos + 1; // causal int num_tiles = (kv_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int tile = 0; tile < num_tiles; ++tile) { int kv_start = tile * Bc; int tile_len = min(Bc, kv_len - kv_start); // Load K tile: each thread loads tile_len elements in strided pattern // Better: each thread loads its dimension for all KV positions // Thread tid loads K[kv_start + j][tid] for j in [0, tile_len) for (int j = 0; j < tile_len; j += 1) { half k_val = k[kv_base + (kv_start + j) * D + tid]; smem_k[j * D + tid] = k_val; } __syncthreads(); // Compute scores for this tile // For each KV position j in tile for (int j = 0; j < tile_len; ++j) { half k_val = smem_k[j * D + tid]; float dp = q_val * __half2float(k_val); // Warp reduction for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { dp += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp, offset); } if (tid % 32 == 0) { warp_sums[tid / 32] = dp; } __syncthreads(); if (tid == 0) { smem_score[j] = (warp_sums[0] + warp_sums[1] + warp_sums[2] + warp_sums[3]) * scale; } __syncthreads(); } // Compute tile max if (tid == 0) { float tile_max = -1e10f; for (int j = 0; j < tile_len; ++j) { if (smem_score[j] > tile_max) tile_max = smem_score[j]; } smem_score[tile_len] = tile_max; // store max at end } __syncthreads(); float tile_max = smem_score[tile_len]; // Update m and l float m_new = fmaxf(m, tile_max); float factor = expf(m - m_new); o *= factor; l *= factor; m = m_new; // Load V tile for (int j = 0; j < tile_len; ++j) { half v_val = v[kv_base + (kv_start + j) * D + tid]; smem_v[j * D + tid] = v_val; } __syncthreads(); // Accumulate output for (int j = 0; j < tile_len; ++j) { float p = expf(smem_score[j] - m_new); l += p; half v_val = smem_v[j * D + tid]; o += p * __half2float(v_val); } __syncthreads(); } // Final normalization and write o /= l; out[q_base + q_pos * D + tid] = __float2half(o); } ``` Wait, there's a subtle issue. In the score computation loop, I'm doing `__syncthreads()` inside a loop over j. But actually, since all threads participate in computing scores for all j, and the score for j depends on a reduction across all threads, the `__syncthreads()` after storing to smem_score is needed. But the loop over j inside has `__syncthreads()` which is fine as long as all threads reach it the same number of times. But there's an issue: the warp_sums write and read could have a race without proper sync. Let me fix: ```cpp for (int j = 0; j < tile_len; ++j) { half k_val = smem_k[j * D + tid]; float dp = q_val * __half2float(k_val); for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { dp += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp, offset); } if (tid % 32 == 0) { warp_sums[tid / 32] = dp; } __syncthreads(); if (tid < 4) { smem_score[j] = warp_sums[tid] + warp_sums[tid + 1] + ...; // can't do this directly } ``` Actually for the cross-warp reduction: - 4 warp partial sums stored in shared memory - Thread 0 can sum them: `smem_score[j] = (warp_sums[0] + warp_sums[1] + warp_sums[2] + warp_sums[3]) * scale;` This is fine. But I need `__syncthreads()` after the warp_sums write by lane 0 of each warp, and then thread 0 reads them. The current structure is: 1. Each thread computes dp 2. Warp shfl reduces to lane 0 3. Lane 0 writes to warp_sums[wid] 4. __syncthreads() ensures all writes are visible 5. Thread 0 reads all 4 and writes to smem_score[j] 6. __syncthreads() ensures score is visible to all Yes, this is correct but there are 2 __syncthreads() per j. With tile_len=64, that's 128 __syncthreads() per tile. That's a lot of barriers. Optimization: compute multiple scores using a single sync. We can: 1. Compute dp for all j in the tile, storing partial sums in registers 2. Do warp reductions for all j 3. Write all warp_sums to shared memory 4. One sync 5. Thread 0 computes all scores 6. One sync But storing dp for all j requires tile_len registers per thread. With tile_len=64, that's 64 floats per thread = 256 bytes. With 128 threads = 32KB registers just for this. With other registers, might still be okay (255 max per thread, 255*4=1020 bytes). Actually, let me think differently. You don't need to compute scores for all j first. FlashAttention-style online softmax needs the tile max before accumulating. So we do need a first pass over the tile to get max, then a second pass to accumulate. Actually, we can compute max incrementally: - For each j, compute score[j] - Update running max - But for FlashAttention, we need the max of the entire tile before the second pass However, if we compute score[j] one by one, we can update online softmax as we go. That's the classic online softmax: m_new = max(m_old, score[j]) factor = exp(m_old - m_new) output *= factor l *= factor p = exp(score[j] - m_new) output += p * v[j] l += p m_old = m_new With this, we don't need to find the tile max first! We can process each KV position sequentially. So the kernel simplifies to: ```cpp for each tile: load K tile load V tile for j = 0 to tile_len: compute score[j] via warp reduction // online softmax update float s = score[j]; float m_new = fmaxf(m, s); float factor = expf(m - m_new); o *= factor; l *= factor; float p = expf(s - m_new); half v_val = smem_v[j * D + tid]; o += p * __half2float(v_val); l += p; m = m_new; ``` This is great! It eliminates the need for storing all scores and doing a max pass. But there's still the issue of computing score[j] with warp reduction. We can optimize the reduction sequence: - For each j, we need one warp reduction (31 shfl ops + 1 shfl per warp for cross-warp) - Or we can use a cooperative group reduction Actually, we can amortize the reduction cost by processing multiple K rows per iteration. But let's keep it simple for now. I'll also increase Bc to 128 to reduce the number of tiles. Wait, can we load V in the same shared memory as K to save shared memory? This is the original two-pass approach. 2. Or load K and V for the tile simultaneously (2x shared memory). 3. Or load K[j] and V[j] on demand (not through shared memory). Here's the improved kernel: ```cpp __global__ void gqa_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int B, int H, int Hkv, int S, int D, float scale) { int b = blockIdx.z; int h = blockIdx.y; int q_pos = blockIdx.x; int tid = threadIdx.x; int lane = tid % 32; int wid = tid / 32; int kv_h = h / (H / Hkv); int64_t q_base = ((int64_t)b * H + h) * S * D; int64_t kv_base = ((int64_t)b * Hkv + kv_h) * S * D; float q_val = __half2float(q[q_base + q_pos * D + tid]); float m = -1e10f; float l = 0.0f; float o = 0.0f; const int Bc = 128; __shared__ half smem_k[Bc * 128]; __shared__ half smem_v[Bc * 128]; __shared__ float warp_sums[4]; int kv_len = q_pos + 1; int num_tiles = (kv_len + Bc - 1) / Bc; for (int tile = 0; tile < num_tiles; ++tile) { int kv_start = tile * Bc; int tile_len = min(Bc, kv_len - kv_start); // Cooperative load of K tile and V tile // Each thread loads Bc elements per array // Thread tid loads: for j = tid, tid+128, ... within [0, tile_len) for (int j = tid; j < tile_len; j += blockDim.x) { int global_kv = kv_start + j; smem_k[j * D + tid] = k[kv_base + global_kv * D + tid]; smem_v[j * D + tid] = v[kv_base + global_kv * D + tid]; } __syncthreads(); // Process each KV position in the tile for (int j = 0; j < tile_len; ++j) { float dp = q_val * __half2float(smem_k[j * D + tid]); // Warp reduction #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { dp += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, dp, offset); s = warp_sums[0]; // Online softmax float m_new = fmaxf(m, s); float factor = expf(m - m_new); o *= factor; l *= factor; float p = expf(s - m_new); o += p * __half2float(smem_v[j * D + tid]); l += p; m = m_new; } __syncthreads(); } o /= l; out[q_base + q_pos * D + tid] = __float2half(o); } ``` This is cleaner. Let me write a much simpler kernel: for each (batch, head, q_seq_pos), compute causal attention using a single thread block where threads are parallel over the head_dim, and the block loops over kv positions. This is straightforward. ```cpp __global__ void gqa_simple_kernel(...) { int b = blockIdx.z; int h = blockIdx.y; int q_pos = blockIdx.x; int d = threadIdx.x; int kv_h = h / (H / Hkv); float q_val = __half2float(q[...]); float max_score = -1e10f; // First pass: compute max score for (int kv = 0; kv <= q_pos; ++kv) { float k_val = __half2float(k[...]); float score = q_val * k_val * scale; // wait, need to sum over d } } ``` But a single thread can't compute the dot product alone. Threads need to cooperate. Let me try a cooperative approach where all threads first load Q, then for each KV position, load K and compute warp-reduced dot product: ```cpp __global__ void causal_gqa(...) { int b = blockIdx.z; int h = blockIdx.y; int q_pos = blockIdx.x; int tid = threadIdx.x; int lane = tid % 32; int kv_h = h / (H / Hkv); int D = 128; int64_t q_idx = ((int64_t)b * H + h) * S * D + q_pos * D + tid; int64_t kv_base = ((int64_t)b * Hkv + kv_h) * S * D; float qf = __half2float(q[q_idx]); __shared__ float smem[128]; float m = -1e10f; float l = 0.0f; float o = 0.0f; // For each KV position, all threads load K and compute dot for (int kv = 0; kv <= q_pos; kv++) { float kf = __half2float(k[kv_base + kv * D + tid]); smem[tid] = qf * kf; __syncthreads(); // Reduce: sum smem[0..127] // Each warp sums 32 elements float part = 0.0f; for (int i = lane; i < D; i += 32) { part += smem[i]; } // Warp reduce for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { part += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, part, offset); } float score; if (lane == 0) { smem[0] = part * scale; } __syncthreads(); score = smem[0]; // Online softmax float m_new = fmaxf(m, score); float f = expf(m - m_new); o *= f; l *= f; float p = expf(score - m_new); float vf = __half2float(v[kv_base + kv * D + tid]); o += p * vf; l += p; m = m_new; } o /= l; out[q_idx] = __float2half(o); } ``` Wait, there are multiple issues: 1. `__syncthreads()` inside the loop with `smem[0] = part` from lane 0 - all threads reach it. 2. But in the reduction phase, `for (int i = lane; i < D; i += 32)` sums 4 elements per thread (D=128, warp size=32, 128/32=4). That's fine. 3. Then warp shuffle reduces to lane 0. Then lane 0 writes to smem[0]. Sync. Then all threads read smem[0]. This should be correct! And there are only 2 __syncthreads() per KV position. With 132 SMs and ~8 blocks per SM (1024 threads), we have ~1000 blocks active at once. Total waves = 65536 / ~1000 = ~65 waves. At ~1ms per wave? Let's see. Actually, each block does ~256 iterations. Per iteration: - 1 global load for K (coalesced, ~10 cycles) - Store to smem (~10 cycles) - Sync (~20-50 cycles) - Read smem + sum 4 floats (~20 cycles) - Warp shuffle (~20 cycles) - Sync (~20-50 cycles) - 1 global load for V (coalesced, ~10 cycles) - FMA and exp (~50 cycles) Total: ~200-300 cycles per iteration. At 2GHz, 256 iterations = ~12800-19200 cycles = ~6.4-9.6 microseconds per block. With 65536 blocks, at 1000 concurrent blocks, ~66 waves * 8 us = ~528 us. Plus overhead. Yes, no bank conflicts! Actually, let me verify: with 32 banks of 4 bytes each, address `smem[i]` maps to bank `(i * 4) % 128 / 4 = i % 32`. So thread lane reads smem[lane], smem[lane+32], etc. All in the same bank for a given warp, but since they're sequential accesses in time (the for loop), not simultaneous, there's no conflict. Wait - actually in the for loop, a thread does `part += smem[lane];` then `part += smem[lane + 32];` etc. These are sequential per thread, so no bank conflict. But wait, all threads in a warp execute the for loop in lockstep. In iteration 0, all threads read `smem[lane]` simultaneously. Thread 0 reads bank 0, thread 1 reads bank 1, ..., thread 31 reads bank 31: NO BANK CONFLICTS! In iteration 1, thread 0 reads bank 0 (smem[32]), thread 1 reads bank 1 (smem[33]): NO CONFLICTS. This is perfect! Then the warp shuffle reduction is also fast. The only issue is: I read smem array after writing to it. In the cooperative load phase, all threads write to smem. Then __syncthreads ensures visibility. Then all threads read. Good. But I need to handle the case where blockDim.x < D. I'm setting blockDim.x = 128, so tid ranges 0..127, and we directly map tid to d. This is fine. However, with batch=4, num_heads=32, seq_len=512: - Grid = (512, 32, 4) = 65536 blocks - Block = 128 threads 65536 blocks is a lot. H100 has 132 SMs, each can run up to 32 blocks. So max concurrent blocks = 4224. Total waves = 65536 / 4224 ≈ 15 waves. At ~10 us per block (estimate), total time might be 150 us. That's actually very reasonable! But there's another concern: block 0 (q_pos=0) does 1 KV iteration, block 1 does 2 iterations, ..., block 511 does 512 iterations. The total work is O(S^2). Longer blocks will take longer. Block 511 takes ~512 * 300 cycles = 153600 cycles = 76.8 us. Blocks near the end dominate. Total kernel time might be dominated by the last few blocks. In a wave, the longest block determines wave time. If we have 4224 concurrent blocks and need 15 waves, and the last wave has blocks with q_pos around 480-511, each taking ~70-80 us. Total time ≈ 80 us. Very fast! So this simple kernel might actually be quite fast for seq_len=512. Let's implement it. Actually, there's a concern about register usage. With 128 threads, each holding several floats, we might hit register limits. Each thread needs: - qf, kf, vf, part, score, m, l, o, f, p, m_new - That's about 11 floats = 44 bytes - Plus loop indices and other temporaries - Maybe ~20 registers per thread - 128 threads * 20 regs = 2560 registers per block - H100 allows up to 64K registers per SM, so we could run many blocks concurrently. So register pressure is very low. This kernel will have excellent occupancy. Let me rewrite it. Also, I'll make the grid 3D: [S, H, B] to minimize launch overhead. One more optimization: instead of storing `part` and reducing via a for loop, I can use direct indexing: ```cpp smem[tid] = qf * kf; __syncthreads(); float part = smem[lane] + smem[lane + 32] + smem[lane + 64] + smem[lane + 96]; // warp reduce for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { part += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, part, offset); } ``` With 128 threads = 4 warps, each warp computes its own sum. The 4 warps then need to be combined. We can do this with: ```cpp if (lane == 0) { smem[warp_id] = part; // warp 0 overwrites smem[0..3] } __syncthreads(); float total; if (tid == 0) { total = (smem[0] + smem[1] + smem[2] + smem[3]) * scale; smem[0] = total; } __syncthreads(); total = smem[0]; ``` This only uses the first 4 elements of smem for the cross-warp reduction. The rest are used for the partial products. Wait, I was using smem for both the partial products AND the cross-warp reduction. That's fine as long as they don't overlap during use. After all threads write partial products and sync, warps read partial products and compute local sums. Then lane 0 writes to smem[warp_id]. Then tid 0 reads all 4 and writes back. Total smem usage = D floats + 4 floats = 132 floats = 528 bytes. Very small. Actually, I don't even need the first full D array. Each thread can directly reduce its lane's elements across warps... no wait, each thread only has access to one partial product. We need to access all partial products to compute the dot product. Here's the clear flow: ```cpp __shared__ float smem[132]; // 128 for partials, 4 for warp sums // Each thread computes q[d] * k[d] and stores in smem smem[tid] = qf * kf; __syncthreads(); // Each warp sums 32 elements spaced by 32 float part = smem[lane] + smem[lane + 32] + smem[lane + 64] + smem[lane + 96]; part += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, part, 16); part += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, part, 8); part += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, part, 4); part += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, part, 2); part += __shfl_down_sync(0xffffffff, part, 1); if (lane == 0) { smem[warp_id] = part; // Uses indices 0..3 } __syncthreads(); float score; if (tid == 0) { score = (smem[0] + smem[1] + smem[2] + smem[3]) * scale; smem[0] = score; } __syncthreads(); score = smem[0]; ``` This is very clean. Then: ```cpp float m_new = fmaxf(m, score); float factor = expf(m - m_new); o *= factor; l *= factor; float p = expf(score - m_new); float vf = __half2float(v[kv_base + kv * D + tid]); o += p * vf; l += p; m = m_new; ``` After the loop: ```cpp o /= l; out[q_idx] = __float2half(o); ``` The kernel is simple and elegant. Let me write this version. I also want to make sure the global memory accesses for k and v are coalesced: - `k[kv_base + kv * D + tid]`: for a fixed kv, threads with different tid access consecutive addresses. Coalesced! - Same for v. For q: - `q[q_idx]`: each thread in a block reads a different dimension for the same (b, h, q_pos). Consecutive threads read consecutive memory. Coalesced! For output: - `out[q_idx]`: same pattern. Coalesced! This kernel should be very efficient memory-wise. Let me also consider using `__ldg` for read-only caching, but H100 has a large L2 cache, so regular loads should be fine. One more thing: I should make sure PyTorch tensors are contiguous. The `forward` wrapper ensures this. Let me write the final code. Wait, there's one subtlety: with `blockIdx.x = q_pos`, the total number of blocks in x-dim is seq_len = 512. In CUDA, the maximum grid.x is very large (2^31-1), so no problem. Actually wait, looking at the original compile timing out, let me think if there's anything in my C++ bindings that could cause issues. ```cpp torch::Tensor gqa_forward_cuda(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v, float scale) { ... causal_gqa_kernel<<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()), ... ); return out; } ``` One issue: `q.data_ptr<at::Half>()` might not compile if `at::Half` is not the correct type. Actually in torch/extension.h, `data_ptr<at::Half>()` is valid for fp16 tensors. But the return type is `at::Half*`. Casting to `half*` should be fine since they're typically bit-compatible. Actually, a safer approach is: ```cpp half* q_ptr = reinterpret_cast<half*>(q.data_ptr()); ``` Without the template parameter, `data_ptr()` returns `void*`. This is safer. Let me use `q.data_ptr()` and cast. Another thing: I need to check CUDA errors after kernel launch for debugging. But for submission, I shouldn't add error checking that might print. Let me write a clean version now.
